The accumulating report-grade dataset behind [[flagship-report]], coded to [[case-template]]. Every figure carries a source or an explicit `unknown`. Net-assets-to-opex ratios are **upper bounds** (net assets include real property), per [[claims-register]] C4. Flagship cases may also get a full standalone note (e.g. [[fisher-sfmoma]]).

**Systematic-unknown finding (process):** `true_endowment_usd` (990 Schedule D Part V) and `fte_headcount` (990 Part I line 5) cannot be pulled from ProPublica's JS-rendered view; they need the downloadable 990 **PDF/XML** (IRS bulk XML is the scalable fix). Expect these `unknown` across many US cases until a PDF/XML pass is run — this holds across all 44 cases now indexed here, not just the 24 previously tracked, which sharpens rather than changes the finding. This itself feeds the report's data-availability chart.

## Primary-verification backlog
Cases coded from WebSearch-snippet synthesis because WebFetch returned `EGRESS_BLOCKED` on every domain attempted (ProPublica Nonprofit Explorer, institution sites, The Art Newspaper, Artnet, GuideStar, Wikipedia) in the research session that coded them. Every quantitative figure in these files is tagged `secondary` even where the underlying document (typically an IRS 990/990-PF) would normally warrant `primary`. Listed oldest-coded first; the standing instruction is to upgrade the 1–2 oldest entries to primary sourcing at the start of each run, before coding anything new, whenever WebFetch is actually working that session. This is the fourth consecutive run confirming a blanket network-egress block for this session/environment, not a domain-specific one. No upgrades happened this run; per the standing instruction (step 5), four more cases were coded via WebSearch-only synthesis and added to the list rather than removed from it.

## Batch 8 (2026-08-16, sixth run) — 50 of 50: one indexing gap fixed, two cases close the "building vs. collection" comparison set

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org and www.moma.org — **both `EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the seventh consecutive confirmation of a session-wide restriction. Per the standing instruction, this run led with depth, not new breadth: (1) it discovered, via `ls cases/*.md` + `grep 'status: coded'`, that [[moma]] (coded 2026-08-16 earlier the same day, before this run) had never been folded into this master file — the exact failure mode Batch 6 fixed for 20 other cases, caught early this time; (2) it used WebSearch (still the only working research channel) to upgrade [[rubin-museum]] and [[moma]] from `unknown`/single-sourced financial fields to real, sourced (if still `secondary`) FY2024 990-derived figures — not a true primary-verification upgrade (no direct fetch happened), but a genuine improvement over guessing or leaving fields blank; (3) with the set at 48/50 after indexing MoMA, it coded two final cases — [[cam-raleigh]] and [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]] — chosen because [[rubin-museum]]'s own file explicitly named both as the sample's missing comparators for the "building vs. collection" question, closing a gap the dataset had already identified rather than picking an arbitrary new case. **This reaches 50 of 50 coded.** Per the standing instruction, future runs shift entirely to depth: primary-source verification (once/if WebFetch unblocks) and original cross-case analysis — no more new cases unless a genuine gap surfaces.

### 48. The Museum of Modern Art — New York, NY · Spot-verified · full note [[moma]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** (three collectors founded a new public institution; founding gifts **absorbed** into a ~200,000-work whole, the coherence opposite of [[barnes]]) · founders **Lillie P. Bliss, Mary Quinn Sullivan, Abby Aldrich Rockefeller** (+ founding trustees) all deceased · **survived_founder YES** (founded 1929, thriving ~96 years) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **independent board from founding** (multiple founders, never founder-sole) · **purpose-built** (53rd St, expanded repeatedly, most recently 2019) · coherence **absorbed** · decision **collective founders/trustees** · constraints **partial/unusual** (the 1931 Bliss bequest's deaccession clause explicitly *permitted* selling her works to upgrade the collection — donor intent designed for adaptability, not preservation; funded the *Les Demoiselles d'Avignon* acquisition).
Numbers: **FY2024 (ended June 30, 2024): net assets ~$2.2B, total expenses ~$299.0M, total revenue ~$324.7M** [MoMA FY24 consolidated financial statements / Form 990, EIN 13-1624100, WebSearch-surfaced, secondary] → **~7.4x [UPPER BOUND]** — by a wide margin the largest net-assets figure in the sample (>3.5x Terra's $614.5M successor foundation, the prior largest). true_endowment/FTE/attendance **unknown**.
Sources: moma.org/about/mission-statement/history · press.moma.org Bliss exhibition release · artnews.com (Rockefeller/MoMA founding) · MoMA FY24 financial statements & Form 990 (WebSearch-surfaced, not directly fetched)

### 49. CAM Raleigh (Contemporary Art Museum / Contemporary Art Foundation) — Raleigh, NC · Spot-verified · full note [[cam-raleigh]]
pathway **hybrid** (existing downtown building since 2011 → sale 2026) · **founder n/a** — civic/institutional origin, no collector-founder, the same schema gap [[michener-art-museum]] hits · **survived_founder n/a** · outcome **distressed** · durability **weak** · governance **independent-board** (Contemporary Art Foundation Inc., EIN 56-1376155) · **adapted-other** → likely **no-building**/smaller footprint · coherence **n/a** (kunsthalle model, no permanent collection — same taxonomy-boundary case as [[onera]]) · decision **foundation-executive** · **friction funding-gap**, explicitly stated as cause, not inferred · constraints no.
Numbers: temporarily closed **June 2025**; building (tax value ~$11M) listed for sale **Aug 2026**, CBRE declining to set an asking price. Revenue fell from **~$900K (2018) to under $570K (2025)** [WUNC, citing tax filings, secondary] — the clearest sourced trend explaining the closure. **Net assets contested between two aggregator snippets ($5.19M vs $2.0M for the same EIN/FY)**, not reconciled this session, flagged rather than picked. Expenses ~$927K (FY2024). FTE/attendance/true endowment unknown.
Sources: wunc.org/arts-culture/2026-08-03/cam-raleigh-building-for-sale · axios.com/local/raleigh/2026/08/04 · wral.com (Aug 2026) · EIN 56-1376155, WebSearch-surfaced aggregator snippets, not directly fetched

### 50. Robert Rauschenberg Foundation — real-estate divestment — New York, NY / Captiva Island, FL · Spot-verified · full note [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]]
pathway **found-foundation** · founder **Robert Rauschenberg** deceased (d.2008) · **survived_founder yes** (18 yrs, well-capitalized, actively grantmaking) · outcome **stable** (the Foundation; the real-estate divestment is the coded event) · durability **strong** ($515M+ net assets, ~32x its own expenses) · governance **independent-board** (no family-continuity option — Rauschenberg had no children, structurally like [[fondation-beyeler]]) · **adapted-residence** (381 Lafayette St., NoHo, his home/studio since 1965; + a 22-acre Captiva, FL compound) → trending **no-building** for these two specific properties · coherence **n/a** (grantmaking/archival body, not a collection-coherence case) · decision **foundation-executive** · **friction funding-optimization**, explicitly *not* distress (contrast [[cam-raleigh]] directly) — a schema gap, since `primary_friction`'s vocabulary has no clean value for voluntary real-estate optimization from strength · constraints no.
Numbers: net assets **$515,221,785**, total expenses **~$16.2M** → **~31.8x [UPPER BOUND]** [ProPublica EIN 65-0200989, WebSearch-surfaced, secondary]. Captiva compound sold to a resort operator for **$45M** (2026); NYC townhouse listed via Newmark, **no asking price disclosed**. true_endowment/FTE/attendance unknown.
Sources: artnews.com (NYC HQ listing) · artforum.com (NYC HQ sale plans) · news.artnet.com (Captiva sale) · EIN 65-0200989, WebSearch-surfaced ProPublica/aggregator snippet, not directly fetched

**New pattern this batch: the sample now has two billion-dollar-plus net-assets cases, from structurally different governance types.** MoMA's ~$2.2B FY2024 net assets is the second case in the sample to cross $1B, after [[glenstone]]'s $2.70B (990-PF) — Glenstone remains the larger figure, correcting an earlier draft of this note; MoMA is not the new ceiling, it is the second data point at the ceiling. What makes the pair useful isn't which is bigger — it's that they arrive at comparable scale through structurally different governance (Glenstone: family-controlled 990-PF, a single living-founder gift; MoMA: independent multi-founder board, public-charity 990, near a century of accretion). Both are financially unassailable, so neither's survival is a money question — a cleaner test of the report's central "governance, not money, predicts survival" claim than either extreme alone, since the interesting variable here is elsewhere (coherence, decision rights, adaptability, per Pattern 10's 990 vs 990-PF caveat on ratio softness).

**New pattern this batch: "building vs. collection" is now a four-point spectrum, not a binary.** With [[cam-raleigh]] and [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]] coded, the sample's comparison set on this question is complete for now: **CAM Raleigh** (distressed, no endowment, no founder — gave up the building because it had to); **Rubin Museum** (well-capitalized, living-founder, gave up its *entire public-facing museum form* — the building was the institution); **Rauschenberg Foundation** (extremely well-capitalized, deceased-founder, divesting real estate that was never the public program at all — grants and archives continue undisturbed); **Deutsche Bank** (healthy corporate parent, narrowing a dedicated venue while keeping the collection). Four cases, four different relationships between "the building" and "the thing that actually mattered," and the current schema (`outcome_category`, `primary_friction`) cannot yet distinguish them without reading each narrative — this is now a confirmed, four-case-deep schema-granularity gap, not a one-off observation.

**New pattern this batch: `primary_friction`'s vocabulary has no value for divesting real estate from strength.** [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]] and, to a lesser extent, [[rubin-museum]] both needed to force-fit `funding-gap`/`none-documented`/a prose caveat onto a friction that is neither acute distress nor "no friction at all" — a deliberate, healthy-position reallocation. Worth a controlled-vocabulary addition (e.g. `strategic-reallocation`) before the schema locks, now that three cases (Deutsche Bank, Rubin, Rauschenberg) share this exact gap.

## Batch 9 (2026-08-17, seventh run) — depth-only: backlog narrowed via WebSearch, first fully-counted governance × survival table

**Process note.** The set is already at 50/50 (reached Batch 8), so per the standing instruction this run did no new case coding at all. WebFetch was re-tested against projects.propublica.org and www.moma.org — **both `EGRESS_BLOCKED` again**, the eighth consecutive run confirming the restriction. With direct primary fetches still unavailable, this run split its effort two ways: (1) WebSearch-only attempts to narrow three flagged backlog contradictions (see the Primary-verification backlog note above [[cam-raleigh]], [[pearlman-foundation]], [[kreeger-museum]] — real progress, but none reaches `primary` tier without a direct fetch, so none is removed from the backlog); (2) the run's main effort, a genuine first for this dataset — actually counting, rather than narratively asserting, the sample's central governance claim across every case that carries the needed fields. See Pattern 14, below.

**New pattern this batch (14): the governance-beats-endowment claim, counted for the first time across the full deceased-founder set, and it holds — with an honest asterisk.** Every prior run asserted Pattern 1 case-by-case, in prose, as new examples arrived. This run instead pulled `governance_control_at_founding` and `survived_founder` from all 50 case files directly (not from memory or a re-read of the prose patterns) and restricted to the only sub-sample the report's own methodology says is testable: cases where the founder is now deceased **and** `survived_founder` resolves to a real `yes`/`no` (excluding `not-yet-testable` living-founder cases, `n/a` corporate/civic cases with no founder to survive, and [[michener-art-museum]], excluded on principle even though technically deceased-founder/yes, because its founder was never a controlling collector-founder in the first place — a civic-founded museum with `governance_control_at_founding: independent-board` from day one and no founder-control question to test).

That leaves **26 cases**. Split by `governance_control_at_founding` at the moment of founding:
- **Not founder-sole at founding** (`independent-board` / `family-controlled` / `mixed` / `parent-institution`) — 13 cases: [[souls-grown-deep]], [[fisher-sfmoma]], [[phillips-collection]], [[menil-collection]], [[shelburne-museum]], [[corcoran-gallery]], [[di-rosa]], [[sid-richardson]], [[dali-museum]], [[hillwood]], [[storm-king]], [[moma]], [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]]. **13 of 13 survived their founder (100%).**
- **Founder-sole at founding** — 13 cases: [[terra]], [[weisman]], [[fisher-landau]], [[kreeger-museum]], [[fondation-beyeler]], [[vogel-collection]], [[de-la-cruz-collection]], [[barnes]], [[broad]], [[chinati]], [[grounds-for-sculpture]], [[pyramid-hill]], [[pearlman-foundation]]. **10 of 13 survived (77%)**: [[weisman]], [[kreeger-museum]], [[fondation-beyeler]], [[vogel-collection]], [[barnes]], [[broad]], [[chinati]], [[grounds-for-sculpture]], [[pyramid-hill]], [[pearlman-foundation]]. **3 of 13 did not**: [[terra]], [[fisher-landau]], [[de-la-cruz-collection]].

Overall: 23 of 26 (88%) survived their founder in this hand-selected set — **not a population rate**, the sample is deliberately picked for pathway/outcome variance, not randomly drawn, and n=26 is small. But the internal comparison is the useful part: cases that started with *any* non-founder-sole governance never failed at the founder's death, while founder-sole-at-founding cases failed 23% of the time in this sample — and every one of the 10 founder-sole survivors independently supports the report's more precise claim (governance *design*, not the founding-moment label alone, predicts survival): [[weisman]] and [[pearlman-foundation]] moved to independent/mixed boards; [[fondation-beyeler]] and [[grounds-for-sculpture]] converted to independent boards years before death; [[kreeger-museum]]'s succession was architectural, not board-based; [[vogel-collection]] and [[broad]] have no institution-shaped failure point to begin with (pre-executed dispersal; 990-PF lending library); [[chinati]]'s permanence doctrine pre-decided the discretionary questions a successor board could fight over; [[pyramid-hill]] had no family successor at all yet still had a board with functioning hiring authority; [[barnes]] only "survived" via a court cy pres override that broke the founder's own deed, arguably the sample's most qualified `yes`.

**Caveat that keeps this honest, not just tidy:** the 3 founder-sole failures are not one clean mechanism either. [[terra]] is the textbook case — founder-sole control, no independent board, no succession plan, litigated collapse at death. [[de-la-cruz-collection]] is close kin — no board or 501(c)(3) structure at all, immediate discretionary liquidation by a surviving spouse days after death. But [[fisher-landau]] is a different phenomenon the coded field partly obscures: the Center closed in **2017, six years before** Emily Fisher Landau's 2023 death, a founder-controlled choice made while she was still living, not a post-death governance vacuum — it is coded `survived_founder: no` correctly by the field's literal rule (didn't continue after death), but it isn't evidence for "founder-sole governance causes a death-triggered collapse" the way Terra and De la Cruz are. Treat the clean version of this run's finding as **2 of 13** death-triggered founder-sole collapses, not 3, with Fisher Landau as a related but structurally distinct third failure mode already named in Batch 2's pattern notes (founder-controlled closure while still living, disposition left to a later estate process).

**Methodology note for future runs:** this table should be re-derived, not hand-copied, whenever a new deceased-founder case is coded, since it is now a real, checkable claim rather than a narrative impression — re-run the same `governance_control_at_founding` × `survived_founder` pull across all `status: coded` files and recompute before publishing any version of this pattern in [[flagship-report]].

## Batch 7 (2026-08-16, fifth run) — the missing distressed-not-bankrupt corporate cell, a misindexed dispersal fixed, and the first living-founder building closure

Three new cases (45th–47th coded overall). **Process note: WebFetch was re-tested this run** against projects.propublica.org and, new this run, apps.irs.gov (the IRS's own Tax Exempt Organization Search, tried specifically to resolve [[pearlman-foundation]]'s missing EIN) — **both returned `EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the sixth consecutive run confirming a session-wide network-egress restriction, not a domain-specific one, and now confirmed against an IRS-owned domain, not just aggregators and third-party institution sites. Per the standing instruction, no primary-verification upgrade was possible; all three cases were coded via WebSearch snippet synthesis, tagged `secondary` throughout, and added to the Primary-verification backlog above (items 19–21). Cases were chosen deliberately: (1) [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] fills the exact gap Batch 5's synthesis flagged as the corporate sub-sample's missing cell — an M&A absorption of a *distressed-but-not-bankrupt* parent, distinct from Lehman/Enron's bankruptcies, Deutsche Bank's voluntary retreat, and JPMorgan Chase's unaffected continuity; (2) [[pearlman-foundation]] fixes a real error in this file's own Pattern 8, which previously asserted the Pearlman dispersal was "already in the sample" when no case file for it existed — and turns out to carry a genuinely novel disposition mechanism (a standing three-museum mutual-loan consortium) worth naming on its own terms, not just as a Vogel/Daskalopoulos footnote; (3) [[rubin-museum]] is the sample's first **living-founder** institution to voluntarily close its own building while remaining financially healthy, filling a gap next to Deutsche Bank's corporate version of the same "building vs. collection" question with a much closer structural match to the report's core museum/foundation case type.

### 45. Credit Suisse Art Collection → UBS — Zürich, Switzerland · Spot-verified · full note [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **hybrid** (whole-collection absorption into acquirer's own larger corporate collection, ongoing integration/partial-disposition) · **founder n/a — corporate collection** · **survived_founder n/a**, recoded **parent-entity-continuity: no** — the sample's first corporate case where the parent itself ceased to exist (emergency, government-brokered UBS takeover, completed June 12, 2023), distinct from bankruptcy liquidation (Lehman, Enron) or voluntary retreat (Deutsche Bank) · outcome **pivoted** · durability **moderate** · governance **parent-institution** (same decision_owner schema gap as every other corporate case, now 6 for 6) · **no-building** · coherence **broad-survey**, with a reported Swiss-artist-heavy pocket (Hodler, Vallotton) mismatched against UBS's own globally-curated collection — a coherence *mismatch at merger*, not a drift within one collection · decision **no-identifiable-person** (operational lead named: Mary Rozell) · **friction collection-incoherence** (mismatched software, largely German-language inventory with incomplete data) · constraints no.
Numbers: **~13,500 works** absorbed; combined UBS collection reported **40,000+ works** post-merger [dimsumdaily.hk, Zarastro Art, secondary]. Absorbed collection also carries legacy non-fine-art material (posters, model ships) from Credit Suisse's own 1980s First Boston acquisition — corporate collections layering through M&A, same mechanism this sample sees in family collections layering through marriages/generations. Disposition mechanism: internal employee sales at discount, proceeds reportedly to charity [single-sourced, secondary]. No 990-equivalent for either bank; UBS's own collection-founding narrative (1970, Donald B. Marron) is itself likely a conflation with the separate PaineWebber lineage absorbed in 2000 — flagged, not asserted as fact.
Sources: dimsumdaily.hk/ubs-grapples-with-credit-suisse-art-collection-integration · zarastro.art/ubs-strategy-integrating-credit-suisse-art-collection · theartnewspaper.com/2023/06/13 & /2023/03/24

pathway **intentional-dispersal** (+donate-existing-museum-scattered) · founder **Henry Pearlman** deceased (d.1974; Rose Pearlman's death date unconfirmed) · **survived_founder yes** (51 yrs Foundation operation post-death before this final disposition) · outcome **dispersed** · durability **strong** (dispersal-structural, like Vogel) · governance **founder-sole → independent** (board composition largely unconfirmed) · **no-building** (49-yr single-host loan at Princeton, now ended) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (Cézanne-centered Impressionist/Post-Impressionist/early-modern European) · decision **foundation-executive** (a clean fit — unlike the corporate/civic cases, this one doesn't hit a schema gap) · constraints partial (general custodial-access mission documented; specific trust clauses unread).
Numbers: **63 works** in the final 2025 gift — 29 to Brooklyn Museum, 28 to MoMA, 6 to LACMA [institutional press releases, secondary]. **Distinctive mechanism**: the three recipients have agreed to a standing **mutual-loan consortium**, lending to each other when works aren't on view at home — a third, genuinely novel dispersal architecture in the sample (distinct from Vogel's unconnected 51-recipient placement and Daskalopoulos's two-museum shared *title*). Foundation established **1955**, 19 years pre-death — a possible fourth pre-death governance lock-in (with Kreeger, Fondation Beyeler, Storm King), unconfirmed absent the trust instrument. **EIN not located this run** — a structural gap distinct from the usual "EIN known, filing unpulled" backlog pattern; several unrelated, similarly-named Pearlman family foundations surfaced instead.
Sources: brooklynmuseum.org/press/pearlman-foundation-gift · news.artnet.com/art-world/pearlman-collection-donation-brooklyn-museum-moma-lacma-2674349 · artmuseum.princeton.edu (49-yr loan history)

### 47. Rubin Museum of Art / Shelley & Donald Rubin Cultural Trust — New York, NY → no fixed location · Spot-verified · full note [[rubin-museum]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** (2004–2024) → **pivoted** to traveling/loan model · founders **Shelley & Donald Rubin** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (founders living; closure was a founder-era decision) · outcome **pivoted** · durability **strong** (not distress-driven — reported $150M+ endowment) · governance **founder-sole → mixed** · **adapted-other** (converted former Barneys building) **→ no-building** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (Himalayan/Tibetan Buddhist art) · decision **foundation-executive** · **friction poor-attendance/funding-gap**, but framed as strategic reach-vs-cost calculation, not acute distress · constraints no.
Numbers: museum closed **Oct 6, 2024** after 20 years; **~4,000 objects** retained, reorganizing as a "global museum without walls" (traveling exhibitions, partnerships, digital access, small NYC research office kept) [CNN, Tricycle, secondary]. Building purchased **1998 for $22M**; a widely-repeated "$21.5M" sale figure could **not** be reliably confirmed as the Rubin's own building rather than an adjacent property this run — flagged open, not resolved. EIN **22-6799567 confirmed** (unlike Pearlman) but the filing itself unpulled — a clean, high-value primary-verification target once WebFetch works. **The sample's first living-founder, non-distressed building closure**, a fourth "building vs. collection" shape alongside CAM Raleigh, Rauschenberg Foundation HQ (both [[candidate-universe]] Signals rows, uncoded), and Deutsche Bank's corporate version.
Sources: tricycle.org/article/rubin-museum-closing · cnn.com/2024/02/02/style/rubin-museum-closing · crainsnewyork.com/real-estate/rubin-museum-shutter-and-sell-longtime-chelsea-home

**New pattern this batch: the corporate sub-sample's predicted fourth cell arrives, and it's messier than the other three.** Credit Suisse → UBS confirms the report's own prior-run prediction (Batch 5, Pattern 9) that a distressed-but-not-bankrupt parent absorption would behave differently from bankruptcy liquidation, voluntary retreat, or unaffected continuity — and it does: not a clean survival or a clean failure, but an ongoing multi-year integration complicated by mismatched cataloguing systems and a coherence mismatch between acquirer and acquired collection. The corporate sub-typology is now a confirmed four-way split (forced liquidation / voluntary retreat / unaffected continuity / distressed-absorption), each with exactly one case — worth naming as a typology, not yet a distribution.

**New pattern this batch: dispersal is not one mechanism, and Pearlman adds a third distinct architecture.** [[vogel-collection]] scatters into 51 independent, unconnected recipients. [[daskalopoulos]] creates shared *title* between two museums (the Guggenheim/MCA Chicago co-ownership). [[pearlman-foundation]] adds a third shape the sample hadn't seen: three independent owners bound by an ongoing, standing mutual-loan obligation to each other — not one distribution event but a permanent circulating consortium. The report's current vocabulary (`intentional-dispersal`, `donate-existing-museum-scattered`) doesn't yet distinguish these three architectures from each other; worth a sub-typology once more dispersal cases accumulate. This is also a live correction: this file previously (Pattern 8, below) asserted Pearlman was "already in the sample" when it was not — a reminder to verify `[[wikilink]]` references against `ls cases/*.md` before publishing a claim about the sample's own composition, the same discipline the Batch 6 reconciliation exists to enforce.

**New pattern this batch: a living-founder, well-capitalized museum can give up its building for the same strategic reason a struggling one gives up its building, and the coded fields can't yet tell the two apart.** [[rubin-museum]]'s $150M+-endowed, non-distressed closure and CAM Raleigh's funding-strained closure ([[candidate-universe]] Signals row, uncoded) would likely code to similar `outcome_category`/`primary_friction` values despite opposite financial starting points. The report's data-availability finding (Pattern 4) already tracks how rarely durability is *measurable*; Rubin adds a second, related finding worth its own line: even where financial health *is* known, the current schema doesn't cleanly separate "gave up the building because it had to" from "gave up the building because it chose to, strategically, from strength" — both currently read as `pivoted` with a funding-adjacent friction tag.

## Batch 11 (2026-08-18, ninth run) — depth-only: two backlog contradictions narrowed via WebSearch, art finance domain advanced

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested at the start of this run against six domains — projects.propublica.org, apps.irs.gov, www.guidestar.org, www.instrumentl.com, en.wikipedia.org, and www.taxexemptworld.com — **all six returned `EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the tenth consecutive run confirming the restriction is session-wide, not domain-specific. Per the standing instruction, no primary-verification upgrade was possible; this run instead used WebSearch (the only working channel) to narrow two flagged backlog contradictions before touching anything else: (1) **[[marciano]]**'s two-vintage net-assets contradiction ($49.6M vs "~$50.3M") — a fresh WebSearch pinned the "~$50.3M" figure precisely to the **FY2024 Form 990-PF** ($50,204,272 net assets / $6,570,728 revenue / $4,637,604 expenses, via ProPublica's own indexed page), narrowing the earlier $49,557,394 figure to a probable FY2023 snapshot — the same "different fiscal years, not a same-year error" resolution already established for [[cam-raleigh]]. Still `secondary` (no direct fetch), but the contradiction is now dated rather than open. (2) **[[jpmorgan-chase-collection]]**'s unconfirmed "began selling parts in the 2020s" claim — a second, independent WebSearch again found no sale/auction/deaccession record and again surfaced the same "rarely sold, occasionally given to museums" characterization from a different source (myartbroker.com's bank-collections survey, corroborating the original curator quote). The claim remains flagged as unconfirmed, not refuted, and stays out of every coded field — absence of a discoverable record is not proof of absence. Neither item reaches `primary` tier and neither is removed from the backlog; both moves are genuine narrowing, consistent with how prior blocked-WebFetch runs have used WebSearch. ## Batch 13 (2026-08-19, eleventh run) — a fifth corporate disposition mechanism, the acquirer's-side UBS case, and the sample's first Central European museum

Four new cases (51st–54th coded overall). **Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested at the start of this run against projects.propublica.org — **`EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the thirteenth consecutive run confirming the restriction. Per the standing instruction, this run led with depth before breadth: **[[weisman]]** (backlog item #2, the oldest never-touched entry — item #1, [[sid-richardson]], was narrowed last run) had its ~$105.5M net-assets figure corroborated against a second independent source and dated to the Form 990-PF filed October 23, 2024, and Billie Milam Weisman's continuous 31-year directorship since Frederick Weisman's 1994 death was confirmed — a genuine narrowing, not an upgrade to `primary`. The bulk of this run's effort then split between four new cases, chosen deliberately to close gaps the pattern-tracking had already flagged: **[[ubs-art-collection]]** supplies the acquirer's-side view of the merger [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] (coded Batch 7) had already coded from the absorbed side, and is the sample's clearest "active steward" corporate case (a healthy, continuously operating collection, contrasted with every other corporate case's crisis or near-crisis); **[[fondazione-prada]]** fills the "corporate-foundation-to-public-museum model at scale" gap [[candidate-universe]]'s own Discovery-cycle-2 notes had flagged as missing, and adds a fourth funding architecture to the `found-foundation` pathway (ongoing corporate-sponsorship-funded, distinct from personal-endowment, postal-clerk-salary, and corporate-collection-owned); **[[muzej-lah]]** is the sample's first Central European case and a genuinely small, non-mega-donor new-region museum, extending the new-region-private-museum pattern the candidate universe has tracked informally in Kazakhstan, Thailand, and Wales; **[[readers-digest-wallace-collection]]** is a historical (1998) corporate art sale that supplies a fifth, previously-missing corporate disposition mechanism — see the pattern below. All four are added to the Primary-verification backlog above (items 25–28), tagged `secondary` throughout.

### 51. UBS Art Collection — Zürich, Switzerland / global · Spot-verified · full note [[ubs-art-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **hybrid** (no-building corporate display + a dedicated 2019 public gallery + an active museum-lending program) · **founder n/a — corporate collection**, contested founding narrative (UBS cites 1971/Donald Marron, very likely a PaineWebber-era conflation absorbed via UBS's 2000 PaineWebber merger) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **parent-institution**, same decision_owner schema gap as every other corporate case, now 7 for 7 · **no-building** + one dedicated gallery (2019, Midtown Manhattan) · coherence broad-survey · decision **no-identifiable-person** (best fit; a named Global Head, Mary Rozell, has held the role since 2015) · **friction none-documented**.
Numbers: collection size contested between **~30,000 works** (a Rozell-era interview, read here as the pre-Credit-Suisse-merger baseline) and **40,000+** (UBS's current marketing page, read here as the post-2023 combined total already coded in [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]]) — this case treats ~30,000 as UBS's own standalone figure to avoid double-counting the same works across two case files. All financial fields **unknown** — no separable art-program budget disclosed; UBS is a Swiss bank with no US-style 990 disclosure regime.
Sources: ubs.com/global/en/our-firm/art · moma.org/calendar/exhibitions/118 · artadvisors.org (Mary Rozell profile) · thegeniuslist.com (Rozell interview)

### 52. Fondazione Prada — Milan / Venice, Italy · Spot-verified · full note [[fondazione-prada]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **found-foundation** (+found-standalone-museum, Milan campus 2015) · founders **Miuccia Prada & Patrizio Bertelli** living, co-chairing since 1995 · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (33-year-old foundation, no founder-death transition) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** (funded by an ongoing sponsorship relationship with a currently profitable, Hong Kong-listed parent company, not a fixed endowment) · governance **founder-sole**, trending mixed (a 2023 advisory Guidance Committee, the sample's first documented step toward broadening a corporate-foundation's decision base proactively rather than in response to distress) · **purpose-built** (OMA/Koolhaas, Milan, 2015) + adapted (Venice palazzo, 2011) · coherence **broad-survey** (art, architecture, philosophy, cinema — a deliberately multidisciplinary mission, not a single-medium thesis) · decision **founder-alone**, trending toward **philanthropic-advisor** · **friction none-documented** · constraints no.
Numbers: founded **1993**; Prada Group's own investor-relations page describes the Foundation explicitly as **"a sponsorship of Prada Group"** — a funding architecture with no clean analogue elsewhere in the `found-foundation` pathway [pradagroup.com, secondary]. Film Fund carries a disclosed **€1.5M annual endowment**, a single program line, not the Foundation's total budget, which was not located. All balance-sheet fields **unknown** — no US 990, no located Italian filing, and the Foundation's own permanent-collection size (as distinct from its exhibition/programming scope) is itself unresolved, a taxonomy-boundary question similar to [[onera]]'s.
Sources: fondazioneprada.org/mission-en · pradagroup.com/en/perspectives/sponsorships/fondazione-prada.html · en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fondazione_Prada

### 53. Muzej Lah — Bled, Slovenia · Spot-verified · full note [[muzej-lah]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founders **Igor & Mojca Lah** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (museum not yet opened at coding) · outcome **emerging** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole** (Fundacija Lah) · **purpose-built** (David Chipperfield Architects, 5,000 sq m, opening announced summer 2026) · coherence **coherent-multi** (post-1945-to-present Slovenian + international, Kiefer/Beuys/Kabakov/Mušič/Imhof named) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open** (founders living, museum not yet opened).
Numbers: **800+ works by 100+ artists**, assembled over ~three decades [Domus, ArtNews, secondary]. Fundacija Lah legally established **2017**, registered in **Lucerne, Switzerland** — not Slovenia, despite the museum, collection subject matter, and founders' business base all being Slovenian, a legal-home/cultural-home split worth tracking as more European cases are coded (contrast [[fondation-beyeler]], where the legal and physical home are the same country). All financial fields **unknown** — no figures for construction cost, endowment, or budget found in any source.
Sources: domusweb.it (Chipperfield design) · artnews.com/art-news/news/collectors-igor-and-mojca-lah-open-contemporary-art-museum-slovenian-mountains-1234744346 · fundacijalah.com/about-us · sloveniatimes.com (Lah family wealth ranking)

### 54. Reader's Digest (Wallace) Collection — Chappaqua / Pleasantville, NY · Spot-verified · full note [[readers-digest-wallace-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **sell-auction** · founder **Lila Acheson Wallace** deceased (d.1984; collection assembled as a corporate office-decoration program, not a personal collection later donated) · **survived_founder yes** in the narrow sense (the collection stayed intact 14 years post-death before the *company* — not any family successor — chose to sell part of it) · outcome **closed-dispersed** (for the sold tranche) · durability **failed** (for the sold tranche) · governance **parent-institution** · **existing-institution** (Reader's Digest's own Pleasantville HQ) · coherence coherent-multi (Impressionist/Modern/contemporary) · decision **no-identifiable-person** · **friction funding-gap**, of a specifically **pre-crisis, anticipatory** character — sold in 1998, eleven years before the company's first (2009) of two bankruptcies · constraints no.
Numbers: **$86.6M** total, Sotheby's Nov 1998, 37 of 39 consigned lots sold (Modigliani $15.1M, Giacometti $7.7M, van Gogh $4.4M, auction records for both Modigliani and Giacometti at the time) [Deseret News/AP, secondary]. The sold tranche represents **well under 1%** of the collection's reported ~8,000-piece total — the fate of the remaining **~7,960+ works is entirely unknown**, the largest unresolved "missing collection" gap in the sample, proportionally larger than [[fisher-landau]]'s comparable gap.
Sources: deseret.com/1998/11/17/19412541 · cnn.com/books/news/9809/16/readers.digest.reuters · cnbc.com/2020/03/02 (company history) · abi.org (2013 bankruptcy)

**New pattern this batch: the corporate sub-sample's disposition mechanism is now a five-way split, and the fifth is the first that is neither crisis nor its absence.** [[report-dataset]]'s existing typology covered forced bankruptcy liquidation (Lehman, Enron), voluntary retreat from a venue while keeping the collection (Deutsche Bank), unaffected continuity through repeated M&A (JPMorgan Chase, and now [[ubs-art-collection]]), and distressed-parent absorption (Credit Suisse→UBS). [[readers-digest-wallace-collection]] adds **anticipatory monetization**: a company that was declining but not yet insolvent sold part of its art asset for cash eleven years before its first bankruptcy filing. This is a meaningfully different mechanism from Lehman/Enron, where the sale *was* the bankruptcy process; here the sale was a proactive lever pulled by a company still trying to avoid that fate. Five corporate cases now show five distinct mechanisms, each with exactly one case, still a typology, not yet a distribution — worth watching as more corporate cases accumulate whether "anticipatory monetization" recurs or was Reader's Digest-specific.

**New pattern this batch: corporate collections narrate their own founding history the same imprecisely as family collections do, and this sample can now show it from both sides of one merger.** [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] (Batch 7) flagged that UBS's own "founded 1971 by Donald Marron" origin story is very likely a conflation with the separate PaineWebber-era program, absorbed only via UBS's 2000 PaineWebber acquisition. This run's [[ubs-art-collection]] case confirms the same contradiction appears in UBS's *own current marketing materials*, not just secondary press synthesis — upgrading it from "a research gap" to "a genuine finding about how corporate collections compress their own layered, M&A-driven history into a single clean founding date that likely isn't accurate." This directly parallels [[report-dataset]] Pattern 8's finding about family collections layering through marriages and generations; corporate collections do the same thing through mergers, and, like family collections, tend to tell a simpler story about it than the record supports.

**New pattern this batch: a `found-foundation` case can be well-capitalized without holding any capital of its own.** Every prior `found-foundation` case in the sample runs on some form of independent capital — a personal endowment (Beyeler, Chinati, Glenstone), a postal-clerk's salary spent entirely on art with no institutional capital at all (Vogel), or a corporation's own balance sheet (the bank cases). [[fondazione-prada]] is a fourth shape: a legally independent public museum, with a real founder-decider, funded entirely through an ongoing **sponsorship** relationship with a separate, currently profitable corporate parent (Prada Group) that the founders also happen to control — real institutional independence, but no independent capital base, a dependency this sample has not yet seen tested, since Prada Group's own fortunes have not (yet) turned the way Reader's Digest's did.

**New pattern this batch: a European collection's legal home and cultural home can be two different countries, and the schema's single `geography` field can't represent that.** [[muzej-lah]] is the second sample case (after [[fondation-beyeler]]) to register its governing foundation in Switzerland, but the first where the museum, the collection's own subject matter (a meaningful share of named artists are Slovenian), and the founders' business base are all in a *different* country (Slovenia) than the legal entity (Lucerne). Whether this is a tax-efficiency, estate-planning, or simple business-domicile choice is unconfirmed, but it is now a real pattern, not a one-off, worth a dedicated note (not a schema change yet, at n=2) as more European cases are coded.
