## Preliminary patterns (144 of ~75/~100 cases coded, PROVISIONAL)
**Updated 2026-09-03 (forty-fourth run, second run today).** Three new cases this run (Batch 44, below) — set grows to 144. New cross-case pattern this run: [[mowaa]] is this dataset's first case with genuinely no individual collector-founder to code at all (a multi-institutional charity project, not a personal collection in transition), and it surfaces a failure mechanism this dataset had never tracked despite 143 prior cases — professional, multi-stakeholder, independent-board governance, backed by real institutional money (the British Museum, the German government, the Mellon Foundation), stalled anyway by a dispute entirely external to the institution's own governance: a traditional monarch's contested authority over the underlying cultural patrimony and land. [[sakip-sabanci-museum]] and [[pinto-art-museum]] close two clean geographic gaps (Turkey and the Philippines) and add, respectively, a founder who created both a university and its museum in one act, and this dataset's first physician-founder. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-09-03 (forty-third run). Three new cases this run (Batch 43, above) — set grows to 141. New cross-case pattern this run: a genuinely new pathway-timing dimension — [[gochman-family-collection]] compresses the collector-to-public-institution arc to five years while [[warehouse-wieland]] (coded the same run) takes 14 years for the same `found-standalone-museum` pathway, and both are still faster than this sample's typical multi-decade norm; worth tracking `institution_open_year` minus `transition_year` (or `collecting_start_year`) as an explicit pace variable once enough cases carry both dates cleanly. [[museum-macan]] closes the sample's Southeast-Asia-beyond-Singapore gap and adds a fourth country (after Switzerland/[[fondation-beyeler]] and Slovenia/[[muzej-lah]]) showing a foundation established ahead of the building's public opening. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-09-02 (forty-second run, second run today). Three new cases this run (Batch 42, above) — set grows to 138. New cross-case pattern this run comes from a recount, not a new case: a full `pathway` × `outcome` retally at 2x the original sample size (138 vs. the 70 cases H3's finding was first drawn from) found the built-institution-vs-lighter-model success-rate gap holds up even more tightly (93.9% vs. 92.3%, narrowed from ~2 points to ~1.6) — but surfaced an unplanned, genuinely new pattern in the same pass: the fast-growing `hybrid`/branched-pathway category (now 26 cases) fails at a materially higher rate (78.9%) than either clean-pathway bucket, a candidate signal that branching itself may be a symptom of unresolved transitions rather than a neutral coding artifact (see Batch 42's Part D writeup, above, and [[what-we-now-believe]]). Separately, [[mennello-museum]] adds a third distinct flavor of `governance_control_at_founding: parent-institution` (municipal, not university), and [[bonovitz-collection]] extends the H2 insider-exception pattern to a ninth confirming case while surfacing a disclosure shape this dataset had not coded before: a private gift with no intermediate foundation or filing vehicle of any kind. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-09-02 (forty-first run). Three new cases this run (Batch 41, above) — set grows to 135. New cross-case pattern this run: Pattern 9 (corporate-collection survival tracks parent balance-sheet health) gets its most extreme test yet in the opposite direction from HSBC's layoffs test — [[monte-dei-paschi-collection]] shows a corporate collection surviving not just headcount pressure but a full state bailout and 68% government ownership stake intact, while [[lehman-brothers-collection]] (already in the sample) shows the same severity of distress, resolved via bankruptcy liquidation instead of recapitalization, destroying the collection entirely. Read together, the two cases isolate the actual mechanism behind Pattern 9: it is the legal resolution path (liquidation vs. rescue), not distress severity, that determines a corporate collection's fate — directly feeding this run's Part D disconfirmation pass on H6/H8 (see [[what-we-now-believe]]). Separately, [[schnitzer-family-foundation]] adds a fourth `traveling-program` case and the first built by an individual founder rather than a bank or foundation partnership, and [[malba-costantini]] adds a founder-driven collection-merger mechanism (a living museum-founder personally acquiring and folding in a wholly separate collection) that the current pathway vocabulary has no dedicated value for — flagged alongside the Lehman/Michener schema-coverage gaps. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-09-01 (fortieth run, second run today). Three new cases that run (Batch 40, above) — set grows to 132. New cross-case pattern this run: the sample's geographic gap in Africa and the Gulf finally breaks — [[barjeel-art-foundation]] and [[standard-bank-art-collection]] are the sample's first Emirati and African cases respectively, and [[yemisi-shyllon-museum-of-art]] gives the university-partnership pathway (Pattern 11) a fourth variant, a founder who pre-funds the host institution's own operating risk directly (a ~$1.7M, ten-year subsidy), rather than trusting the university's general funds the way [[ogden]]'s case shows can later sour into conflict. This run's Part D disconfirmation pass is directly relevant to that same case: a fresh hunt found evidence (Queens College, CUNY) that even a genuinely wanted, curated university acceptance can still erode into chronic neglect absent sustained funding commitment — sharpening, not yet testing at scale, exactly the risk Shyllon's subsidy structure is a hedge against (see [[what-we-now-believe]]). Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-09-01 (thirty-ninth run). Three new cases that run (Batch 39, above) — set grows to 129. New cross-case pattern this run: Pattern 9 (corporate-collection survival tracks parent balance-sheet health, not ownership/headcount change) gets its sharpest test yet — [[hsbc-art-collection]] has weathered ~35,000 layoffs (2020) plus a further 2024-2026 cost-cutting/AI-restructuring drive targeting up to 20,000 more roles with no located impact on the art program, a larger and more sustained headcount-pressure test than any prior corporate case. [[fondation-pierre-berge-yves-saint-laurent]] adds a genuinely new shape to Pattern 6/H8: a founding couple routing two adjacent legacies to two different pathways (a pre-built foundation for one; deliberate total auction for the other) at the same historical moment, and this dataset's first same-sex-partnership succession case. [[easton-foundation]] extends Pattern 1's pre-death lock-in finding with a 26-years-early instance succeeding to a chosen non-family professional collaborator rather than a recruited independent board — a `decision_owner` shape (see Pattern 6's update) the schema still can't name cleanly. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-08-31 (thirty-eighth run, second run today). Two new cases this run (Batch 38, above) — set grows to 126. New cross-case pattern this run: the corporate sub-sample's geographic concentration finally breaks — [[uob-art-collection]] is its first Asian instance after eleven straight US/European cases, and its founding motive (explicitly supporting a fledgling regional art scene, not building brand-appropriate office decor) reads closer to the corporate-*foundation* cases (Cartier, Prada) than to the other "active steward" bank collections (UBS, JPMorgan, Bank of America) already coded — worth tracking whether a founding-motive sub-typology (support-the-field vs. institutional-asset-building) predicts anything once a second Asian corporate case exists. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-08-31 (thirty-seventh run). Three new cases this run (Batch 37, above) — set grows to 124. New cross-case pattern this run: a sixth corporate-mortality/near-mortality shape, alongside the five already named in Pattern 2 below — [[unicredit-art-collection]] shows a corporate collection's disposition *policy* reversed entirely by a single leadership succession, with no financial distress or M&A event of any kind behind either decision, extending Pattern 6's `decision_owner`-vocabulary gap (see its update below) from "no identifiable person" cases to a case with an identifiable but ill-fitting decision-maker (a named CEO, twice over). [[lee-kun-hee-collection]] extends Pattern 4's data-availability finding with a fourth shape of "unknown," alongside blocked-by-tooling, unmeasurable-in-principle, and not-yet-created: a transfer where no comparable disclosure regime was ever going to apply, a state museum donation rather than a nonprofit filing. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-08-30 (thirty-sixth run, second run today). Two new cases this run (Batch 36, below) — set grows to 121. New cross-case pattern this run: a durability mechanism this dataset had not coded at all — [[price-sculpture-forest]]'s permanent conservation easement binds the *land* itself, through a third-party land trust, independent of the nonprofit's own governance, endowment, or board continuity (see new Pattern 15, below). [[mint-museum]] also extends Pattern 6 (civic/corporate origination stories the `decision_owner` vocabulary undercounts) with a clean new instance: a museum founded by an uncoordinated citizens' coalition, then built up for nine decades by dozens of independent donor bequests with no connecting figure between them at all — see Pattern 6's update below. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-08-29 (thirty-fourth run). Three new cases this run (Batch 34, above) — set grows to 116. New cross-case pattern this run: a fourth H8 constraint-failure mechanism, distinct from the three below (judicial modification, governance vacuum/financial exhaustion, a narrow constraint honored) — [[star-of-hope-foundation]] shows a specific, written founder constraint set aside by a legitimately governed successor board with no litigation, suggesting `constraints_documented: yes` predicts durability only where some party holds standing to enforce it, a dimension not yet coded systematically across the dataset (full result in the Batch 34 section above and [[what-we-now-believe]]'s H6/H8 entry). Numbering below (1-5, plus the numbered patterns further down) has not been re-derived case-by-case since earlier, smaller-count runs and should be treated as historically dated prose illustrating the mechanism, not a live count against the current 116-case set — Pattern 14 (governance × survival table) remains the standing instruction for any actual rate. Older update note retained below for continuity: Updated 2026-08-20 (fourteenth run). Three new cases this run (Batch 16, above) — set grows to 65, still short of the ~75 near-term target, so breadth growth continues. New cross-case patterns this run (founder-sole governance producing a survivable crisis rather than a fatal one when a living-founder-era funding crisis forces a real governance handoff, per [[dia-art-foundation]]; the corporate-sponsorship found-foundation architecture now tested across four decades and two cases; a sixth disclosure-regime shape, an entity that may not be incorporated at all) are stated in full in the Batch 16 section above rather than repeated here. Prior update (2026-08-20, thirteenth run): three new cases (Batch 15) — set grew to 62. New cross-case patterns that run (the "foundation"-in-name-only naming/legal-form mismatch; the H8 refinement from Poldi Pezzoli's 145-year survival alongside a narrow, honored constraint; the second non-collecting-institution taxonomy problem) are stated in full in the Batch 15 section above rather than repeated here. Pattern 14 (governance × survival table, added Batch 9) remains the current authoritative version of Pattern 1 and should be re-derived, not hand-copied, now that more cases exist. Prior update (2026-08-19, eleventh run): four new cases (Batch 13) — set grew to 54, still well short of the ~75 near-term target. New cross-case patterns this run (the corporate sub-sample's five-way disposition-mechanism split; the corporate self-narrated-founding-date finding, now confirmed on both sides of one merger; the sponsorship-funded `found-foundation` variant; the legal-home-vs-cultural-home split for European cases) are stated in full in the Batch 13 section above rather than repeated here; Pattern 14 (governance × survival table, added Batch 9) remains the current authoritative version of Pattern 1 and should be re-derived, not hand-copied, now that 4 more cases exist. **Not conclusions.** The set is hand-selected for variation, not random, so no rates are published off it; these are hypotheses sharpened across the growing designed set — both depth (primary verification, sharper cross-case synthesis) and breadth continue in parallel per Alina's 2026-08-18 direction. Data gaps are named, not hidden.

1. **Governance, not money, decides whether a collection survives its founder — and the mechanism can be architectural or legal, and can lock in decades before the building even exists.** Among the deceased-founder cases, survival is the norm, and the failures locate the cause. Terra had roughly $450M behind it and still failed, its governance was **founder-sole** (the only original directors were the founder and his son), so his death was a control vacuum that became litigation and closure. The survivors consistently had **independent boards and succession set before death**: Grounds For Sculpture's board that predated the founder's death by 20 years, the de Menils, Phillipses, and Havemeyer Webbs all handing to professional successors ([[mcnay]]'s self-perpetuating board is the same shape but is correctly excluded here, per the 2026-08-25 QA audit — `mcnay.md` remains `status: founder-review`, outside the numbered coded dataset, and Pattern 14 below already omits it correctly; this prose line previously cited it in error). **[[kreeger-museum]] added a variant**: its succession was locked in not through a will or board charter but through the building itself — Philip Johnson designed the 1968 house explicitly to convert into a public museum, a physical pre-commitment made 22 years before the founder's 1990 death. **[[fondation-beyeler]] now extends the pattern further**: its governing foundation (a Swiss Stiftung) was established in 1982 — 28 years before Ernst Beyeler's death, and 15 years *before the museum building itself opened* in 1997. That's the earliest and most complete pre-death lock-in the sample has found, and it comes with a distinct twist: Beyeler had no children, so unlike the family-handoff survivors, the succession ran directly to an independent, non-family board (chaired by industrialist Hansjörg Wyss) with no family-continuity option ever on the table. Emerging read, sharpened: **governance design (legal or physical) locked in before death predicts survival better than endowment size or family continuity — the lock-in can precede the institution's own building, and can bypass family entirely.** Test at 50, and check how many other house-museums in the sample (McNay, Phillips, Weisman) had a comparable physical pre-commitment. **This run's reconciliation adds two more independent-board survivors and one genuine complication.** [[storm-king]] is the cleanest multi-generation version yet: Ralph Ogden founded it in 1960, died in 1974, and it passed to his son-in-law H. Peter Stern, then (2008) to Stern's own son John — three consecutive family successions, 66 years, still thriving, the longest continuous family-succession chain in the sample. [[pyramid-hill]] is the inverse test: Harry Wilks installed **no family successor at all** — a founder-sole decision-owner with nobody groomed to follow — yet after his 2014 death the board simply ran a national search and hired an outside executive director in 2019, and the institution survived anyway. Read together with Terra (founder-sole *and* no independent board *and* failed), Pyramid Hill suggests the operative variable isn't family continuity, and isn't even founder-sole decision-making during life — it's specifically whether an **independent board with hiring authority already existed** at the moment of death, family successor or not. **The complication**: [[chinati]] is coded `governance_control_at_founding: founder-sole` — the same value as Terra — and it has thrived for 32 years since Judd's 1994 death, not failed. Chinati and Terra cannot both be explained by "founder-sole governance fails" alone; the difference more likely sits in *what* founder-sole control was exercised over. Judd's permanence doctrine (works installed as fixed, unrotatable ensembles) removed most of the ongoing discretionary decisions a successor board could fight over, while Terra's board faced live, contestable choices (deaccession, location, program) with no settled precedent. Worth testing at 50: does founder-sole governance only predict failure when paired with *unresolved* future discretion, not when the founder's design pre-decides the hard questions?

2. **Two failure modes, on two clocks — plus a corporate-sector clock that now splits into three sub-mechanisms, one of which isn't failure at all, and a fourth that isn't even disruption.** Governance failure kills fast (Terra, on the founder's death). Funding failure kills slow (Corcoran dissolved after 126 years as its endowment eroded below ~0.5x operating coverage; di Rosa distressed and now selling its campus). [[lehman-brothers-collection]] added a third clock — a corporate parent's balance-sheet failure, liquidating an otherwise well-run art program overnight regardless of the collection's own health — and **[[enron-art-collection]] confirmed that clock with a second, independent case** (2001–02 vs. Lehman's 2008–10), while adding a variable Lehman alone couldn't show: timing and cost basis at forced sale (Enron's flagship lot sold at a documented loss against its own purchase price; Lehman's sold above estimate). **[[deutsche-bank-collection]] added a fourth, distinct shape that the report should not fold into "failure" at all**: a financially healthy corporate parent voluntarily retreating from a dedicated public venue while explicitly keeping the collection intact. **[[jpmorgan-chase-collection]] now adds a fifth shape that isn't disruption at all**: a corporate collection that has continued, apparently uninterrupted, through *four separate M&A events of its own parent* (1996, 2000, 2004, 2008) without dispersal or pause — suggesting the operative variable isn't "did the parent change hands" but specifically "did the parent's balance sheet fail" (Lehman, Enron) versus merely change ownership or strategy (JPMorgan Chase's mergers, Deutsche Bank's voluntary pivot). Institutional mortality (and near-mortality) in the corporate sub-sample is now at least three distinct mechanisms — forced liquidation (bankruptcy), voluntary retreat (healthy parent narrows footprint), and unaffected continuity (healthy or even repeatedly-acquired parent, no impact on the collection) — the report should track corporate-collection cases (§8 of [[candidate-universe]]) as their own category with this three-way split, not fold them into the museum/foundation funding-gap story. **[[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] (coded Batch 7) is now that fourth cell**: an M&A absorption of a *distressed*, not bankrupt, parent (UBS's emergency, government-brokered takeover of Credit Suisse, June 2023) — see Batch 7 and Pattern 9 below for how it complicates the clean three-way split.

3. **The eponymous museum is losing share to lighter models, especially among living founders.** The set spans multiple pathways, and the newer/living-founder cases cluster on the alternatives: the Broad's lending library, the Cisneros and Daskalopoulos gift-and-co-ownership dispersals, the Fisher 100-year loan, the Neue Galerie merger, the traveling Kinsey. Consistent with the wealth-transfer thesis, the generation now deciding reaches for models that avoid a standalone institution's cost and fragility. Real shift or selection artifact is a 50-case question. **[[vogel-collection]] shows the same "lighter model" instinct can be a *founding* choice rather than a later pivot, and for a reason that has nothing to do with 21st-century institutional fatigue**: the Vogels never had the wealth to build a standalone museum in the first place (funded entirely from a postal clerk's salary), and total intentional dispersal into 51 existing institutions was their only realistic option — a useful reminder that "avoids a standalone institution's cost" isn't only a strategic choice available to the wealthy; for the sample's poorest founders it's closer to the *only* option.

4. **A data-availability finding the report can own — and it now splits into three distinct shapes of "unknown," not one.** The durability metric everyone wants, endowment coverage, is largely **unmeasurable from public data**: the true endowment (IRS Schedule D) is `unknown` for most cases, the headline net-assets ratios are all upper bounds inflated by real property, and they are meaningless for grantmaking/lending vehicles (Broad ~109x, Longleaf ~111x are non-signals). How rarely a collection's durability can be verified from the outside is itself a finding, and a reason collectors cannot benchmark themselves. This run's batch sharpens the finding into three genuinely different categories that were previously collapsed together: **(a) blocked by tooling** — 15 of 44 coded cases (see Primary-verification backlog, top of file) carry no primary-sourced figures at all because this session's WebFetch tool has been blocked by network egress policy across four separate research runs (2026-08-13, 2026-08-14 first run, 2026-08-14 second run, 2026-08-15 third run — this run's test specifically tried a non-aggregator third-party PDF host, foundationsearch.com, hoping a direct-linked 990-PF PDF might route around the block; it did not, confirming this is a blanket session-level restriction, not a domain- or content-type-specific one); a 990 filing or sale record exists and would resolve the figure cleanly, it just couldn't be pulled directly. **(b) unmeasurable in principle** — [[yuz-museum]]'s finances are `unknown` not because a document is unreached but because China has no 990-equivalent public-disclosure regime for private foundations; no re-fetch will ever resolve this one. **(c) not yet created** — [[benini]]'s Form 990 is `unknown` because the Benini Foundation only received 501(c)(3) status in June 2025 and likely has no first filing yet; this `unknown` will resolve with time, not with better tooling. The report should keep these three apart: only (a) is a fixable artifact of this session's network restrictions; (b) and (c) are honest, durable findings about the field itself.

5. **The living-founder cohort is the report's forward-looking core.** A large share (Glenstone, Crystal Bridges, Magazzino, Bechtler, Audain, Thoma) code as survival **not-yet-testable**, these are exactly the significant collections about to enter the transition the report is about. Analyze survival on the deceased-founder set; frame the living-founder set as the coming decisions, tying the historical data straight to the wealth-transfer moment.

6. **The `decision_owner` and governance sub-fields undercount civic and corporate origination stories.** [[michener-art-museum]] (founded by Bucks County government and an existing arts nonprofit, with the namesake as principal donor rather than founding collector), [[cam-raleigh]] (Batch 8 — no founder at all, a pure civic/nonprofit origin with no collector-founder to code `founder_status_at_transition` or `survived_founder` against), and [[lehman-brothers-collection]] (disposition decided by a bankruptcy estate/creditors' committee, not any person or board) all resist the current vocabulary, which assumes a collector, family, or museum/foundation board sits behind every transition. [[enron-art-collection]] and [[deutsche-bank-collection]] both hit the same wall in the prior run, and **[[jpmorgan-chase-collection]] now makes it five corporate cases with no clean `decision_owner` fit**, still not a rate, but a confirmed and recurring *type* of gap that spans every corporate case coded so far, crisis or not. The report should either name this as an explicit schema limitation or extend the vocabulary (`civic-institutional`, `bankruptcy-estate`, and `corporate-executive` values, at minimum) before the 50-case set is used to characterize "who decides." **[[de-la-cruz-collection]] adds a sharper point on the existing `spouse` value**: the sample's other spouse-decision case, [[weisman]], is a spouse *institutionalizing* a continuation (widow as successor-director inside a standing foundation); De la Cruz is a spouse choosing *immediate, discretionary liquidation* with no institutional intermediary at all. Same coded value, opposite outcome — worth flagging before `decision_owner` distributions get charted, since the field currently can't distinguish "spouse who continues the institution" from "spouse who winds it down." **[[mint-museum]] adds a cleaner instance still of the civic-origination gap**: coded `no-identifiable-person`, it was founded not by any collector but by an uncoordinated 1930s citizens' coalition, then grown for nine decades by a sequence of individually-acting bequest donors (Delhom, Robicsek, Belk-Cook, Bellios) with no shared advisor, family tie, or coordinating figure located between any of them — the receiving-museum-side mirror of H2's usual donor-side question, and a genuinely different shape from the bankruptcy-estate and corporate-executive gaps this pattern already names. **[[unicredit-art-collection]] inverts the gap rather than repeating it**: coded `no-identifiable-person` per the standing corporate-case convention, but the actual decision-maker in both directions (launching the 2019 sell-off, then reversing it in 2021) is a specifically named individual, the bank's CEO, twice over, under two different people holding the title. The current vocabulary's closest values (`museum-director`, `foundation-executive`) don't fit a for-profit corporate CEO either, so this is a case the field genuinely cannot code accurately, not merely a case with no identifiable person at all — worth naming as a distinct sub-gap (an identifiable but uncodeable decision-maker) rather than folding into the same bucket as the bankruptcy-estate and civic-coalition cases, which are genuinely leaderless. **[[fondation-pierre-berge-yves-saint-laurent]] adds a third distinct shape to the `spouse` value**, alongside [[weisman]] (institutionalizing) and [[de-la-cruz-collection]] (discretionary liquidation, no intermediary): Bergé's decision to auction the couple's personal collection fed the proceeds directly back into an institution he and Saint Laurent had already built together, neither pure continuation nor pure liquidation, but both at once across two separate assets. **[[easton-foundation]] surfaces a decision-maker type this pattern hasn't named yet at all**: Jerry Gorovoy, Louise Bourgeois's 30-year studio assistant, fits none of `spouse`, `children`, `independent-board`, or the commercial sense of `art-advisor` cleanly — a chosen professional collaborator with no family tie, coded `art-advisor` only as the nearest available fit, worth its own vocabulary value (`chosen-successor` or similar) before the schema is used to characterize non-family succession at scale.

7. **"Succession locked before death" is not binary — an attempted-but-blocked mechanism is a distinct third case, and the sample's first international case shows why it matters.** [[yuz-museum]] (Shanghai) is the first case where a founder actively pursued a formal succession instrument years in advance — a joint foundation with LACMA, from 2018 — and had it blocked by forces entirely outside his own planning (geopolitics, pandemic timing), yet the institution continued anyway via informal family succession. That sits between Kreeger's *completed* architectural lock-in and Terra's total absence of any attempt; coding it as a flat `unknown` would flatten a real and interesting distinction. Going international at all also surfaces a disclosure-regime gap the US-heavy sample hadn't hit yet: China has no 990-equivalent, so financial durability is unmeasurable there in principle, not just unpulled (see pattern 4c). [[fondation-beyeler]] adds a second, cleaner international disclosure case: Switzerland sits somewhere between the US regime (searchable, if this session's tooling could reach it) and China's (no equivalent at all) — a Swiss foundation's financials exist and are presumably filed with a supervisory authority, but are not searchable/aggregated the way a US 990 is. Three international cases now show three different disclosure regimes; the report's data-availability finding should eventually map degrees of opacity by jurisdiction, not just US-vs-not-US.

8. **NEW: governance can substitute completely for endowment — the sample's zero-endowment case still reads as maximally durable.** Every other case in the sample's durability discussion runs through some size of endowment or net-assets ratio, even when flagged as an upper bound. [[vogel-collection]] has **no endowment at all, ever** — the entire "capital structure" was a postal worker's salary — and yet its disposition mechanism (total, deliberate, pre-death distribution into 51 established institutions, 1992–2009) is arguably the most durable in the whole dataset by construction: no single point of failure, no balance sheet to erode, nothing dependent on any one board or foundation's future decisions. This is the sharpest available test yet of the report's central claim that governance design predicts durability better than endowment size — Vogel is the $0-endowment extreme, and it still reads as durable as, or more durable than, cases with endowments in the hundreds of millions. One case; the mechanism (dispersal into existing, independently-endowed institutions rather than a single new entity) generalizes conceptually to Souls Grown Deep and, since Batch 7, to the now-actually-coded [[pearlman-foundation]] — **correction: an earlier version of this line asserted Pearlman was "already in the sample" when no case file for it existed; it is coded as of Batch 7, and turns out to add a third, distinct dispersal architecture rather than a simple repeat of Vogel's mechanism (see Batch 7 patterns above)** — worth an explicit cross-case comparison now that three genuinely different dispersal architectures (Vogel/unconnected, Daskalopoulos/shared-title, Pearlman/circulating-consortium) exist in the sample.

9. **NEW: for corporate collections specifically, surviving the parent's M&A is not the same test as surviving the parent's bankruptcy.** [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]] is the sample's first corporate case with no crisis at all — 65+ years of continuous operation through at least four ownership-changing events for its parent bank (1996, 2000, 2004, 2008), apparently without the collection itself ever being paused or dispersed. Read against Lehman and Enron (both destroyed by their own parent's bankruptcy) and Deutsche Bank (a healthy parent voluntarily narrowing its public footprint while keeping the collection intact), the emerging read is that **the operative variable for corporate-collection survival is whether the parent's balance sheet actually fails, not whether the parent changes hands or strategy**. This reframes pattern 2's corporate sub-typology as a three-way split (forced liquidation / voluntary retreat / unaffected continuity) rather than the two-way failure-mode framing that works for the founder-death cases. Flagged, one caveat: a claim in [[candidate-universe]] that JPMorgan "began selling parts in the 2020s" could not be corroborated this run and is not treated as fact — see [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]] Narrative.

10. **NEW (reconciliation finding): the net-assets-to-opex ratio can measure the wrong entity entirely, and this bites hardest exactly where a founder deliberately split "who owns the art" from "who runs the public program."** [[kinsey]] is the clearest case: the collection is owned and toured by the family's for-profit-adjacent KBK Enterprises, while the public 990 belongs only to a small educational foundation (FY2024 net assets $649K, opex $354K) — a 1.83x ratio that describes almost nothing about the actual enterprise's durability. [[thoma]] shows the same trap from the opposite direction: two same-family entities exist (a Santa Fe art foundation and a separate Chicago foundation), the ~$1.2B net-assets figure pulled this run sits on one EIN, and until the collection-holding entity is confirmed that figure cannot responsibly be coded as this case's durability signal — it is held `unknown` rather than risk attributing a $1.2B cushion to the wrong balance sheet. Every private-operating-foundation case in the reconciled batch ([[broad]], [[glenstone]], [[magazzino]], [[longleaf]], [[bechtler]], [[thoma]]) shares a milder version of this problem: because a 990-PF operating foundation typically carries its own art collection at book value, its net-assets-to-opex ratio is a *softer* upper bound than a public-charity museum's, not just an equally-soft one — the [[claims-register]] C4 caveat should distinguish 990 (public charity, art often not on the balance sheet at market) from 990-PF (private foundation, art often is). Worth a dedicated methodology note before the 50-case charts ship, since it would otherwise silently overstate durability for exactly the best-capitalized, most family-controlled cases in the sample.

11. **NEW: the university-partnership pathway's first governance-conflict case.** Every university-partnership case coded before this run (Michener) resisted the `decision_owner` vocabulary but was not distressed. [[ogden]] is different: Roger Ogden's 1999 gift to the University of New Orleans produced a thriving regional museum, but state budget cuts to UNO strained the affiliation badly enough that Ogden **sued the UNO Foundation** to move ownership of the art to the museum's own nonprofit — a university partnership turning adversarial over who actually controls the collection, a friction type (`governance-conflict`, specifically donor-vs-host-institution rather than founder-vs-board) the pathway hadn't shown yet. Net assets also *fell* sharply, FY2021's $9.78M to FY2024's $5.68M, an unexplained decline that blocks a firm durability read. One case, but it reframes "hand the collection to an existing institution" as carrying its own distinct failure mode — loss of control to the host — distinct from the funding-gap and governance-vacuum clocks pattern 2 already tracks.

12. **NEW: a taxonomy-boundary case worth naming rather than quietly coding around.** [[onera]] is included specifically because it stresses the schema: David B. Peterson's Onera Foundation is, by its own history, an architectural-preservation advocacy and grantmaking body (the Onera Prize at Columbia predates any public venue) that acquired a landmarked 1836 house and only in October 2025 began showing loan/commission exhibitions in it — there is no owned art collection at all, and `collection_size` is correctly coded `n/a`, not `unknown`. It also could not be located on ProPublica under any name tried, so unlike the 15-case WebFetch-blocked backlog, its financial opacity may be structural (a 990-N filer below the disclosure threshold, or registered under an unrecognized legal name) rather than a tooling failure. As the 50-case set fills in, the report should decide explicitly whether "collection" needs to mean *owned art* to qualify a case for inclusion, or whether stewarding a landmarked building as a public exhibition platform counts — Onera is the test the schema hasn't had to answer yet.

13. **NEW: a fourth national disclosure regime, cleaner than expected.** [[audain]] is the sample's first Canadian case, and it revealed a disclosure gap distinct from the three the sample already tracks (US 990 — searchable; Swiss foundation supervisory filing — exists but not aggregated; Chinese — no equivalent at all). Canada's CRA T3010 is a real, public, per-charity filing (comparable in principle to a US 990) that simply was not pulled this run — a structural cousin of the WebFetch-blocked backlog, but on a different regulator and portal than every other item in it. The museum's own FY2023 figures (expenses ~CAD $8.7M, revenue ~CAD $40.8M) came through a third-party aggregator (charitydata.ca) rather than CRA directly, and the revenue figure is almost certainly a one-time gift inflating it more than 4x above expenses — a reminder that a "primary-adjacent" aggregator figure still needs the disaggregation a raw filing would show plainly.
14. **NEW (2026-08-17, seventh run): the governance-beats-endowment claim, counted for the first time, holds — 13/13 non-founder-sole cases survived their founder vs. 10/13 founder-sole cases (77%), n=26 testable.** Full derivation, the exact case lists, and the honest caveat (the 3 founder-sole "failures" are really 2 clean death-triggered collapses plus one structurally different living-founder closure, [[fisher-landau]]) are in Batch 9, below. This is the first time this pattern was actually tabulated from the coded fields across the full set rather than asserted case-by-case in prose; treat it as the current authoritative version of Pattern 1 and re-derive it (don't hand-copy it) whenever a new deceased-founder case is added.

15. **NEW (2026-08-30, thirty-sixth run): a durability mechanism this dataset's endowment-ratio and board-governance vocabulary cannot see at all — the land itself can be permanently locked, independent of the institution.** Every prior durability discussion in this dataset runs through some combination of endowment size, board governance, or a founder's legal/architectural pre-commitment (Pattern 1). [[price-sculpture-forest]] adds a genuinely different mechanism: founder Scott Price placed a permanent conservation easement on the park's land with the Whidbey Camano Land Trust, a third-party instrument that survives regardless of whether the nonprofit operating the sculpture park itself continues, thrives, or dissolves. Even in a Terra-style governance collapse, the *land* could not be cleared or redeveloped — a durability floor this dataset's coded fields (`governance_control_at_founding`, `net_assets_to_opex_ratio`, `constraints_documented`) have no place to record, since it binds neither the founder's estate nor the institution's board, but a physical asset via an outside conservation body. Worth watching for in future small, land-based cases (art parks especially), and worth a schema note: `constraints_documented` currently conflates founder-imposed governance/display restrictions with this entirely different land-use-restriction mechanism.

---

### First real signal (pilot complete, 9 cases + McNay)
The **survived_founder field is doing exactly the work the report needs**, it separates failure modes that a crude "closed = failed" would flatten:
- **Terra** = did **not** survive the founder (governance vacuum on his death → litigation → closure). Friction: governance.
- **Corcoran** = survived 126 years, then **dissolved on money** (endowment coverage fell below ~0.5x). Friction: funding.
- **di Rosa** = survived, still open, but **distressed and drifting** from founding intent (campus for sale). Friction: funding.
- The 5 thriving/stable survivors (SGD, Fisher, Phillips, Menil, Shelburne) all cleared succession.
Emerging hypothesis to test at scale: **governance design at founding (independent board + succession plan) predicts survival better than collection value or endowment size** — Terra had ~$450M behind it and still failed on governance; the durable ones had board continuity locked before the founder's death. This is H8-adjacent and must be tested across all 50, not asserted from ten. **Do not publish any rate off ten hand-picked cases;** the pilot proves the template codes cleanly, every cell is sourced or honestly `unknown`, and the agents catch false premises (di Rosa).

---

## Batch 14 (2026-08-19, twelfth run) — a governance-transition case announced at founding, a for-profit rejection of the nonprofit form, and a fifth corporate sub-typology cell

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org and www.irs.gov (a fresh domain) — **both `EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the fourteenth consecutive confirmation of the session-wide restriction (see the backlog note below). Per the standing instruction, no backlog upgrade was possible this run (a WebSearch-only check of [[sid-richardson]]'s FY2025 filing found none yet publicly posted — the FY2024 figures already coded remain current, no change). Effort went instead into breadth: four new cases, chosen deliberately for variation the sample lacked entirely — **MONA** is the sample's first Australian case and its first collector-founded museum structured explicitly *outside* the charitable/nonprofit form (a for-profit holding-entity structure, funded by unrelated gambling-business dividends rather than an endowment); **Casabianca** is a non-museum, non-foundation pathway new to the sample (a private collection opened to the public purely as a wing of an existing hospitality business, with no separate legal or financial identity at all); **Bank of America's "Art in our Communities"** fills a real gap the corporate sub-typology had not yet covered, a collection whose entire public-life strategy is free loans to other institutions rather than owning any venue; **Almaty Museum of Arts** is the sample's first Central Asian case and the first to publicly announce a founder-to-mixed-governance succession *intent* at the moment of opening, rather than that trajectory only being visible in hindsight. This reaches **59 of ~75 (near-term target; ~100 eventually) coded**.

### 56. Museum of Old and New Art (MONA) — Hobart, Tasmania, Australia · Spot-verified · full note [[mona-museum]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founder **David Walsh** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong**, but by a mechanism no other case shows: not an endowment ratio, an **ongoing personal dividend subsidy** from Walsh's unrelated gambling business · governance **founder-sole**, deliberately kept outside the charitable form ("no interest in running MONA as a charitable foundation... that would attract tax subsidies") · **purpose-built** (2011, underground, Nonda Katsalidis) · coherence **broad-survey** · decision **collector-alone** · constraints **no**.
Numbers: cumulative losses **AU$408M since 2011** (~AU$25M/yr), covered by **AU$695M+ in dividend income (2019-2025)** from Walsh's other holdings, not an endowment [ABC News, secondary]. Revenue **AU$41.4M (FY2024)**, down from AU$44.9M (FY2023). Attendance **330,000 (FY2024-25)**, Tasmania's 3rd-most-visited attraction. net_assets/true_endowment/FTE **unknown — no comparable disclosure regime** (not a registered charity by design).
Sources: abc.net.au/news/2025-11-15 & 2026-06-05 · theconversation.com (tax treatment) · en.wikipedia.org/Museum_of_Old_and_New_Art

### 57. Casabianca (De Santis family collection) — Lake Como, Italy · Provisional · full note [[casabianca]] · `origin: private-individual`
pathway **hybrid** (private collection, opened to paying visitors, run as a wing of an existing luxury-hotel company — no museum or foundation form at all) · founders **Paolo & Antonella De Santis** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** (opened May 2026) · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole**, no board of any kind — the villa sits inside the same corporate structure as the family's hotels (Grand Hotel Tremezzo, Passalacqua) · **adapted-residence** (1930s villa, Piero Ponci) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (postwar Italian/Arte Povera) · decision **collector-alone** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: **~50 works** on view, **€15 admission** (the only revenue figure found, not disaggregated from the hotel group) [Art Newspaper, secondary]. No separable financials exist — not a distinct legal entity from the family's commercial hotel business, a structural finding, not a sourcing gap.
Sources: theartnewspaper.com/2026/05/06 · grandhoteltremezzo.com/en/gazette · lombardiasecrets.com

### 58. Bank of America Corporate Art Collection ("Art in our Communities") — Charlotte, NC / global · Spot-verified · full note [[bank-of-america-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **traveling-program** (no dedicated venue, ever — the collection's entire public-facing strategy is free loans to other institutions) · **founder n/a — corporate** · **parent-entity-continuity: yes** · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **parent-institution** · **no-building** (unlike UBS's one gallery or Deutsche Bank's now-closing venue) · coherence **broad-survey** · decision **no-identifiable-person** · friction **none-documented**.
Numbers: **175+ free exhibition loans** since the program's 2008 launch; collection size contested **~30,000 vs. ~60,000 works** across two sources, unresolved. No art-program budget or FTE disclosed, consistent with every other coded corporate case. Founding narrative shows the same self-history compression already found for UBS/Credit Suisse — "50+ years" (implying ~1975) vs. a better-documented 1967 predecessor thread (National Exchange Bank of Chicago, Nancy Newhall).
Sources: about.bankofamerica.com/en/making-an-impact/art-in-our-communities · artsandcollections.com · news.mit.edu/1996/museum-loan-1106

### 59. Almaty Museum of Arts (ALMA) — Almaty, Kazakhstan · Provisional · full note [[almaty-museum-of-arts]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founder **Nurlan Smagulov** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** (opened 2025-09-12) · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole at opening**, with a **publicly stated intent** (not yet executed) to move toward mixed governance including state participation — a rare case where the succession trajectory is announced at founding rather than only inferable in hindsight · **purpose-built** (Chapman Taylor, ~$100M project) · coherence **coherent-multi** (700+ Kazakh/Central Asian works + international names) · decision **collector-alone**, transitioning per stated plan toward foundation-executive/board · constraints **open**.
Numbers: building **~$30M** within a **~$100M** total project budget. An **endowment fund** is stated to exist (framed as making the museum "a collective public asset"); size and governance **unknown**. No comparable disclosure regime — Kazakhstan is the sample's fourth non-US structural gap after Switzerland, Canada, and China.
Sources: euronews.com/culture/2025/10/12 · artnet.com (Smagulov interview) · artnews.com · ocula.com

**New pattern this batch: a collector can reject the nonprofit-museum form entirely, and the resulting durability mechanism is unlike anything else in the sample.** Every other found-standalone-museum case, even the most founder-dominated ones (Glenstone, Chinati), is organized as a charitable entity with *some* separation between the founder's personal assets and the institution's. MONA has none: Walsh has stated on the record he does not want the tax-subsidized charitable form, and the museum's AU$408M in cumulative losses are covered indefinitely by dividend income from an entirely unrelated, volatile personal business (a gambling syndicate), not by an endowment or any fund attached to the museum. This decouples the report's usual durability metric (net-assets-to-opex, however flagged as an upper bound) from the outcome entirely: MONA is unambiguously thriving by every visible measure while being financially *unmeasurable* in the sample's own vocabulary — the data-availability finding (Pattern 4) gains a genuinely new shape, "unmeasurable by design," distinct from "unmeasurable because the filing is unpulled."

**New pattern this batch: succession intent can be announced at founding, not just inferred after the fact.** Every prior founder-sole case's governance trajectory (Fondation Beyeler's move to independence, Weisman's broadening board) had to be read backward from what eventually happened, years or decades later. Almaty Museum of Arts states its intended trajectory — founder-sole toward mixed governance with state participation — publicly, at the moment of opening, before any of it has occurred. This is coded `open` under `constraints_documented`, distinct from a binding instrument (`yes`) or no plan at all (`no`), and gives H8 a new, testable data point: a future run, once ALMA has operated long enough, can check whether a *publicly stated* succession intent is more likely to be honored than a private, undocumented one.

**New pattern this batch: the corporate sub-typology gains a fifth cell, the standing-subsidy model.** [[report-dataset]]'s corporate typology already covers forced liquidation (Lehman, Enron), voluntary retreat from a dedicated venue (Deutsche Bank), unaffected continuity (JPMorgan Chase), and distressed absorption (Credit Suisse→UBS). Bank of America's "Art in our Communities" is a fifth, structurally distinct shape: a collection that never built a dedicated venue at all, choosing instead to fund other institutions' exhibition budgets directly via free, fully curated loans — sidestepping the real-estate cost pressure that drives Pattern 5's "building vs. collection" spectrum entirely, because there was never a building to give up.

**New pattern this batch, extending an existing one: corporate self-history compression is now a three-case-deep finding.** UBS, Credit Suisse→UBS, and now Bank of America each compress a genuinely layered, multi-merger collecting history into one clean, marketing-friendly founding narrative that a closer read complicates (UBS's 1971/Marron claim is likely a PaineWebber-era conflation; Bank of America's "50+ years" obscures a better-documented, separately-dated 1967 predecessor thread). Three independent corporate cases now show the identical mechanism [[report-dataset]] Pattern 8 already names for family collections layering through generations — worth stating in the manuscript as a confirmed cross-sector finding, not a one-off observation.

**Revised source tally, all 59 cases: 29 coded entirely from secondary WebSearch-snippet synthesis** (the Primary-verification backlog, below, now 32 items after this run's four additions) · **~22 have at least primary-sourced (direct 990 pull) net-assets/expenses figures** (unchanged this run — WebFetch still blocked) · **10 have no comparable disclosure regime at all and are structurally, not just currently, unresolvable this way** — the prior 9 plus MONA (deliberately structured outside the charitable form; no filing exists to pull, a distinct reason from "wrong jurisdiction"). Almaty joins the "blocked by tooling, not structurally absent" bucket alongside Fondation Beyeler/Muzej Lah/UBS/Credit Suisse-UBS, since a Kazakhstani registry may exist but was not located, not confirmed absent.

## Batch 15 (2026-08-20, thirteenth run) — a second Central Asian schema stress-test, a "foundation"-in-name-only finding, and the sample's oldest surviving case by a wide margin

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org — **`EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the fifteenth consecutive confirmation of the session-wide restriction. Per the standing instruction, no backlog upgrade was possible; effort went entirely to breadth. Three cases were chosen deliberately for variation the sample lacked: **Tselinny Center** deepens the sample's youngest disclosure-regime gap (Central Asia, after [[almaty-museum-of-arts]]) while also stress-testing the schema as a second non-collecting, program-only institution alongside [[onera]]; **Sammlung Boros** is the sample's first appointment-only-forever private museum (no walk-in access has ever existed) and surfaces a new naming-vs-legal-form finding; **Museo Poldi Pezzoli** is a deliberate very-long-horizon historical comparator, a 19th-century bequest-founded house museum that has now operated continuously for 145 years, the longest survival window in the sample by a wide margin. This reaches **62 of ~75 (near-term target; ~100 eventually) coded**.

### 60. Tselinny Center of Contemporary Culture — Almaty, Kazakhstan · Provisional · full note [[tselinny-center]]
pathway **hybrid** (no clean vocabulary fit — non-collecting culture center, the second taxonomy-boundary case after Onera) · founder **Kairat Boranbayev** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** (permanent venue opened Sept 2025) · durability **indeterminate** — thriving programmatically, but resting entirely on one individual's personal legal standing, already tested once · governance **founder-sole** (advisory board only, no fiduciary authority) · **adapted-other** (1964 Soviet cinema, Asif Khan renovation) · coherence **n/a** (explicitly non-collecting by policy) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction founder-control** — Boranbayev was arrested 2022, convicted 2023 on embezzlement/money-laundering charges tied to a gas-supply deal, released on probation after asset forfeiture; the institution reportedly remains inside his private corporate structure rather than being ceded to independent or city ownership · constraints **no**.
Numbers: opened **2025-09-12**; **12,000+ visitors in the first 3 days** (no annual figure located). No comparable disclosure regime — Kazakhstan has no general public financial-disclosure register for private foundations/NGOs, a **confirmed structural absence**, corroborated independently (ICNL, HRW), not merely an unlocated filing. Renovation cost reported only as a hedged, unconfirmed "several million dollars." net_assets/FTE/collection-size **n/a or unknown**.
Sources: tselinny.org (primary, mission/team pages) · cimam.org · kiar.center · kz.kursiv.media · mag.humodoc.com (critical analysis) · designboom.com / archdaily.com (building)

### 61. Sammlung Boros (Boros Collection) — Berlin, Germany · Provisional · full note [[sammlung-boros]]
pathway **found-foundation**, flagged — the institution markets itself as "Boros Foundation" but is legally a **gemeinnützige GmbH** (HRB 116284 B), not a German Stiftung, a distinct entity type with materially less independent oversight · founders **Christian & Karen Boros** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **thriving** (600,000+ cumulative visitors since 2008, fifth rotating "presentation" opened May 2026) · durability **moderate** — thriving visibly, no independent board, no succession plan located, financial disclosure effectively invisible · governance **founder-sole** · **adapted-other** (1943 Nazi-era air-raid bunker, converted 2003–2007) · coherence **coherent-multi** (international contemporary, ~1990–present) · decision **collector-alone** · constraints **no**.
Numbers: opened **2008**; collection size contested **~500 vs. ~700 works**, unresolved. Ticket price **€20 standard / €12 reduced**, no walk-in access ever offered — appointment-only for the institution's entire 18-year public history. No aggregate revenue, net-assets, or FTE figure located; German GmbH statutory filing obligations exist in principle but no filed figures were located (coded `unknown — blocked by tooling or filing threshold`, distinct from Tselinny's confirmed structural absence).
Sources: boros-collection.com (primary) · Northdata (HRB 116284 B, registry, primary) · theartnewspaper.com (2007) · en.wikipedia.org/Reichsbahnbunker_Friedrichstraße · e-flux.com (Fifth presentation, 2026)

### 62. Museo Poldi Pezzoli — Milan, Italy · Spot-verified · full note [[museo-poldi-pezzoli]]
pathway **found-house-museum** (1861 will, opened 1881, two years post-death) · founder **Gian Giacomo Poldi Pezzoli** deceased (d. 1879, heirless) · **survived_founder YES — 145 years**, the sample's longest survival window, exceeding even [[corcoran-gallery]]'s 126 years before dissolution · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **mixed** — a hybrid public/private board (state, region, city, plus heir and association appointees) established by the founder's own will, not retained by family · **adapted-residence** (the founder's own palazzo) · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **collector-alone** (pre-mortem, via will — a clean match to [[mcnay]]) · **friction none-documented** internally; the institution's one major disruption, WWII bombing (Aug 1943), was external, with an 8-year state-funded reconstruction (reopened 1951) · constraints **YES** — the 1861 will bound the museum to maintain Poldi Pezzoli's display system per Brera Gallery rules, a narrow constraint honored for 145 years, unlike Barnes's much broader indenture, eventually broken by a court.
Numbers: **~3,000 objects at founding → ~6,000+ today** [secondary, unreconciled against a primary catalogue] · attendance **~45,000/yr (pre-2023) → ~80,000/yr (2023-on)** [secondary, press] · one located budget line, **MiC contribution ~€167,000 (2024)** [secondary, search-summarized from the foundation's own Rendiconto PDF, flagged for direct re-verification] · net_assets/total_expenses/FTE **unknown** — Italy has no US-990 equivalent, though the foundation voluntarily publishes annual reports on its own "Amministrazione Trasparente" page, a disclosure practice worth noting among the sample's non-US cases.
Sources: museopoldipezzoli.it (primary, history/foundation/transparency pages) · biblioteche.cultura.gov.it (Ministry of Culture register, primary) · milano.zone (2026-08-04) · en.wikipedia.org/Museo_Poldi_Pezzoli

**New pattern this batch: a "foundation" can be a foundation in name only, and the mismatch is itself worth tracking.** [[sammlung-boros]] brands itself "Boros Foundation" while being legally a gemeinnützige GmbH, a corporate form with no equivalent to a German Stiftung's independent supervisory-authority oversight. This extends [[report-dataset]] Pattern 3 (Batch 14) — corporations narrating their own founding *history* more cleanly than the record supports — to a new target: an institution's own *legal form*, not just its founding date, can be branded more durably-sounding than it structurally is. As more non-US private-collection cases are added, "Foundation" in an institution's name should be checked against its actual registry entry, not assumed.

**New pattern this batch: the sample's oldest case complicates a clean reading of H8.** [[museo-poldi-pezzoli]]'s founder did impose a binding constraint (display method, via his 1861 will) — yet the institution has thrived for 145 years, the sample's longest survival window, while [[barnes]]'s much broader indenture (governance, location, and display all locked) required a court override to survive. Read together, the two cases suggest H8's predictive variable may be less "did the founder impose any constraint" and more "how much of governance itself the constraint locked" — Barnes locked governance and location; Poldi Pezzoli's will locked only a display convention and handed everything else to a standing public/private board immediately, at founding. Worth testing further as more historical, non-US house-museum cases are added.

**New pattern this batch: a second non-collecting institution confirms Onera's taxonomy problem is a recurring type, not a one-off.** [[tselinny-center]] joins [[onera]] as an institution the schema struggles to code cleanly because it owns no art collection at all — both are program/exhibition-space-led rather than object-led. Unlike Onera, Tselinny's non-collecting stance is a stated, permanent institutional policy ("never appoint a head curator or amass a collection"), not an evolving position. The report should decide explicitly, before the 75-case set is used to characterize "what a collection becomes," whether non-collecting culture centers belong in the sample at all, or should be tracked as a related-but-distinct comparison class.

## Batch 16 (2026-08-20, fourteenth run) — a permanence-doctrine foundation's near-death while its founder still lived, the true origin case for the corporate-sponsorship foundation model, and the sample's first Southeast Asian case

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against three domains — www.irs.gov, projects.propublica.org, and www.causeiq.com (a fresh nonprofit-aggregator not previously tried) — **all three `EGRESS_BLOCKED`**, the sixteenth consecutive run confirming the session-wide restriction. Per the standing instruction, no primary-verification upgrade was possible; this run's Part A effort went entirely to breadth. Three cases were chosen deliberately: **Dia Art Foundation** is a direct comparator to already-coded [[chinati]] — the sample's first *multi-site, multi-artist, board-controlled* permanence-doctrine foundation, and the first case with a documented near-collapse (1985: founder Heiner Friedrich ousted from his own institution, a Citibank loan secured against stock, 18 works sold at Sotheby's) that happened while a controlling founder was still *living*, not at his death, giving the governance-beats-endowment pattern a new trigger type to test. **Fondation Cartier** fills the row [[candidate-universe]] §8 has carried unaddressed since the corporate-foundation category was created, and turns out to predate already-coded [[fondazione-prada]] by roughly a decade (1984 vs. 1993) — the sample's true origin case for the "corporate sponsorship, no independent endowment" foundation model, mid-relocation into a third purpose-built Jean Nouvel venue (Place du Palais-Royal, opened Oct 25, 2025). **Khao Yai Art Forest** is the sample's first Southeast Asian case, a land-art site (comparator to [[chinati]], [[storm-king]], [[pyramid-hill]]) funded entirely from the Chearavanont family's (Charoen Pokphand Group) personal wealth, with no source located confirming it is even incorporated as a Thai foundation at all — a sixth, distinct disclosure-regime shape this sample has not seen before. This reaches **65 of ~75 (near-term target; ~100 eventually) coded**.

### 63. Dia Art Foundation — Beacon, NY (+ multi-site: NYC, New Mexico, Utah, Germany) · Spot-verified · full note [[dia-art-foundation]]
pathway **found-foundation** (+found-standalone-museum, found-art-park branches) · founders **Heiner Friedrich & Helen Winkler** reported living, **Philippa de Menil** reportedly deceased 2025 but **unconfirmed by a second source** — coded `mixed`/`unconfirmed` rather than a clean `deceased` · **survived_founder not-yet-testable in the clean sense** — the institution already survived a *governance* rupture (Friedrich's 1985 ouster) that functions like a founder-death test without any founder having died · outcome **thriving** (9-12 permanent sites; expanding) · durability **strong** · governance **founder-sole (1974-1985) → independent-board (1985-present)**, a rare **coded governance-form change within a single case**, forced not by death but by a funding-and-regulatory crisis while the controlling founder was alive · **existing-institution** (Dia:Beacon, a converted 1929 Nabisco factory) mixed with site-specific land art (The Lightning Field, Spiral Jetty stewardship) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (Minimalism/Land Art, permanently installed, unrotated — the same "permanence doctrine" as [[chinati]]) · decision **collector-alone → foundation-executive/board** · **friction funding-gap + founder-control** · constraints partial.
Numbers: founded **1974**; 1985 crisis: **$3.8M Citibank loan** against stock, 18 works (Warhol, Twombly, Judd) sold at Sotheby's. Net assets **contested, two unreconciled figures**: $228,603,898 ("as of 2023") vs. $273,547,036 (undated, more recent) [Cause IQ/ProPublica aggregation, secondary] → **~9.9x [UPPER BOUND]** using the higher figure. Total expenses **$27,547,275**; total revenue **$41,856,742** (undifferentiated). true_endowment/FTE/attendance **unknown**.
Sources: diaart.org/about · Gallery 98 institutional history · en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heiner_Friedrich · causeiq.com/organizations/dia-arts-foundation,237397946

### 64. Fondation Cartier pour l'art contemporain — Paris, France · Spot-verified · full note [[fondation-cartier]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **found-foundation** (+found-standalone-museum branch) · founder **Alain Dominique Perrin** (Cartier's president 1975-1998) living, founded the Foundation 1984 at artist César's suggestion, still president · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **thriving** (42 years continuous; 2025 relocation reads as expansion, not retreat) · durability **strong**, with the same corporate-sponsorship-dependency caveat coded for [[fondazione-prada]] — no independent endowment, funded on an ongoing basis by Cartier/Richemont · governance **parent-institution** (a corporate act from the outset, distinct from Prada's `founder-sole` coding) · **purpose-built**, three times over: Jouy-en-Josas (1984) → 261 Blvd Raspail, Jean Nouvel (1994) → 2 Place du Palais-Royal, Jean Nouvel (opened Oct 25, 2025) · coherence **broad-survey** (multidisciplinary "living creation" mission from inception, not a later drift) · decision **founder-alone/parent-institution** (the same `decision_owner` gap Pattern 6 flags, recurring at a founder-who-is-also-company-president level) · **friction none-documented**.
Numbers: founded **1984** — predates [[fondazione-prada]] (1993) by ~9 years and Prada's own venues by up to 21 years, making Cartier the sample's true origin case for the corporate-sponsorship foundation model. Collection size **contested, unreconciled**: "1,500 works/350 artists" vs. "4,500 works/500 artists/60 nationalities" [secondary, plausibly different vintages]. New building **est. €225-245M construction cost** [WWD, Designboom, secondary] — a one-time capital figure, not an operating budget. net_assets/expenses/FTE/attendance **unknown** — no French 990-equivalent, and Richemont's own annual report does not appear to break out a Foundation-specific sponsorship line, the same non-disclosure architecture already coded for Prada Group/[[fondazione-prada]], now a confirmed second case.

### 65. Khao Yai Art Forest — Pong Ta Long, Nakhon Ratchasima, Thailand · Provisional · full note [[khao-yai-art-forest]]
pathway **found-art-park** · founder **Marisa Chearavanont** living (married into the Charoen Pokphand Group family, one of Thailand's wealthiest) · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** (opened Feb 6, 2025) · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole** — land purchased personally in 2022, **no independent foundation, board, or governing body confirmed** in any source · **no-building** (landscape itself; individual pavilions for specific works, e.g. Elmgreen & Dragset's "K-BAR") · coherence **coherent-multi** (Louise Bourgeois, Richard Long, Fujiko Nakaya, Francesco Arena, Elmgreen & Dragset, plus Thai artist Ubatsat) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: site **contested, 161 vs. 210 acres**, unreconciled. Personal collecting "more than 30 years"; site opened 2025. All financial fields **unknown — a likely STRUCTURAL gap, not merely unpulled**: Thailand does have a real foundation-registration and annual-filing regime (Civil & Commercial Code §§110-136), but **no source confirms Khao Yai Art Forest is itself incorporated as a foundation at all** — every account describes personal land ownership and direct family funding. This is a sixth, distinct disclosure-regime shape for the sample (after US 990, Swiss foundation-supervisory, Chinese no-equivalent, Canadian CRA T3010, and the Prada/Cartier corporate-sponsorship-line shape): a real regime exists in the jurisdiction, but the entity may not have opted into it by incorporating at all.
Sources: ArtNews · Designboom · STIRworld · Monocle · ArtReview · Nation Thailand

**New pattern this batch: founder-sole governance can produce a survivable crisis rather than a fatal one, if the crisis forces a real governance handoff while assets still remain.** [[dia-art-foundation]] ran the governance-beats-endowment experiment on itself: 1974-1985 founder-sole control (one person's fortune, no independent capital base) produced exactly the kind of vacuum Pattern 1 associates with failure — except the trigger was a funding/regulatory crisis while the controlling founder was alive, not a death. The board expanded to seven independent members and professionalized in the same 1985 reorganization that resolved the debt, rather than the institution dissolving. This is a genuine variant worth holding next to Terra's *actual* death-triggered collapse: founder-sole control is not a uniform predictor of failure, but of *fragility*, and whether that fragility resolves into a Dia-style recovery or a Terra-style collapse may depend on whether a crisis (of any kind, not only death) forces a governance handoff before assets run out. It also sharpens the standing Chinati/Terra puzzle (Pattern 1's "complication"): Dia's *art* stayed protected by the same permanence-doctrine mechanism Chinati uses (fixed, unrotated single-artist installations), while its *money* still needed an independent board to survive a real crisis — suggesting the permanence doctrine and financial governance are two separate axes Chinati has not yet been tested on the way Dia already has.

**New pattern this batch: the corporate-sponsorship "found-foundation" architecture is now a two-case, four-decade-tested pattern, not a single contemporary example.** [[fondazione-prada]] was coded as a fourth found-foundation funding architecture (alongside personal-endowment, postal-clerk-salary, and corporate-collection-owned). [[fondation-cartier]] is a second, older case with the identical shape — corporate sponsorship, no independent endowment — and because it predates Prada by roughly a decade and has run continuously for 42 years without a distress signal, it changes the read from "one luxury-house example" to "a model that has now been stress-tested across four decades," though neither case has yet been tested by a genuine downturn in its parent's willingness to fund it.

**New pattern this batch: a sixth distinct shape of disclosure-regime "unknown."** The sample's disclosure-regime map already distinguished blocked-by-tooling, unmeasurable-in-principle (China), a real-but-unaggregated regime (Switzerland, Canada), and a corporate-sponsorship-line-inside-a-conglomerate's-accounts shape (Prada, now also Cartier). [[khao-yai-art-forest]] adds a sixth: a jurisdiction (Thailand) with a real foundation-disclosure framework in principle, where the specific case may not have opted into that framework by incorporating as a foundation at all — the gap is about the entity's own legal form, not the country's transparency laws.

## Batch 17 (2026-08-21, seventeenth run) — a national-government sale with a persisting family philanthropic tail, South America's first case with a corrected "loss of control" narrative, and a second university-partnership shape

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against three domains — projects.propublica.org, www.candid.org, and www.grantable.co — with two distinct failure modes (`EGRESS_BLOCKED` on the first and third, DNS failure/`ENOTFOUND` on the second, itself worth noting as a variation on the same underlying restriction), the **seventeenth consecutive run** confirming it. The Sid Richardson backlog item (#1) was independently re-verified via WebSearch this run, producing the same $519,728,605/FY2024 figure already on file — a third independent corroboration, but still not a direct fetch, so still not upgradable to `primary`. Per the standing instruction, this run's Part A effort went to breadth: three cases chosen deliberately. **Museum Berggruen** is the sample's first case of a collection placed directly with a national government rather than an independent nonprofit or foundation — a fourth structurally distinct disclosure-regime shape (state appropriations, no disaggregated collection-level budget) — and surfaces a genuinely new finding: a founder's family continuing to *fund* an institution financially, three generations deep, long after formally losing all ownership and control of it. **Inhotim** is the sample's first South American case, and its research corrected an inaccurate premise carried in this vault's own prior working notes (that the founder had "lost control" through legal/financial distress) — the actual documented sequence is closer to [[dia-art-foundation]]'s pattern, a founder-sole crisis resolved by a *voluntary* governance conversion while living. **Anderson Collection at Stanford** adds a second, structurally distinct university-partnership case alongside [[ogden]] — a purpose-built single-building gift, currently mid-succession at its own founding director's July 2026 departure, a live rather than retrospective test of a founder-connected leadership era ending. This reaches **68 of ~75 (near-term target; ~100 eventually) coded**.

Building cost **contested: $30.5M (2011 budget) vs. $36M (2014 completion)**, unreconciled; who specifically funded the building (family vs. Stanford's broader capital pool) is **unresolved**. net_assets/expenses/endowment/attendance/FTE **unknown — structural**, a university department with no separately disclosed 990-style filing.
Sources: news.stanford.edu · anderson.stanford.edu · stanforddaily.com · sfgate.com · architecturalrecord.com · artforum.com

**New pattern this batch: a founder's family can keep funding an institution long after formally surrendering all ownership and control of it.** Museum Berggruen's founder sold his collection to the German state in 2000, at a price he himself described as a fraction of market value, specifically to see it kept in Germany. That transaction was the full and final transfer of control — no board seat, no veto right, nothing retained as a matter of the sale terms located this run. And yet 21 years later, in 2021, his family voluntarily committed to fund the museum with **€1 million a year starting in 2025**, and his son Nicolas Berggruen holds an ongoing board seat. This is a different shape of founder-legacy persistence than anything else in the sample: not retained control (Barnes, the Bass family at Sid Richardson) and not a clean institutional handoff with no further family role (MoMA's absorbed founders), but a family voluntarily remaining a *funder*, without governance leverage, of an institution it no longer owns.

**New pattern this batch: a "lost control" narrative, checked against the sourced record, turned out to be the wrong mechanism.** This vault's own prior working notes described Inhotim's founder as having lost control of the institute through legal/financial distress. This run's dedicated research found no bankruptcy or judicial-recovery filing specific to the founder personally, and the actual documented sequence — a 2020 court ruling *protecting* the art institute's collection from being used to settle its founder's separate mining-company tax debt, followed two years later by the founder's own voluntary total donation of his remaining personal holdings — reads much closer to [[dia-art-foundation]]'s 1985 pattern (a founder-sole crisis resolved by a governance handoff while living) than to a forced liquidation. Catching and correcting this is itself worth recording: an inherited assumption, even inside this vault's own accumulated notes, is not exempt from the same sourcing discipline applied to any new claim.

**New pattern this batch: the university-partnership pathway hides at least two structurally different sub-models.** [[ogden]] is a living founder's regional-canon collection folded into an existing university museum system. [[anderson-collection-stanford]] is two now-deceased founders' national-scope collection given its own free-standing, purpose-built building, administratively separate from Stanford's existing museum — closer in practice to a standalone museum that happens to sit on university land and payroll than to "a gift absorbed into a university." The schema currently treats both as one pathway value; the two Stanford-and-Ogden comparators together are the first evidence this may need splitting.
