## Batch 18 (2026-08-21, eighteenth run, second run today) — a corporate collection that outlived both its founder and its founding curator, Latin America's second standalone-museum case, and a live gift-restriction-vs-cash-need fight outside the collector-founder frame

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against two fresh domains — projects.propublica.org (re-test) and www.charitynavigator.org (new) — both `EGRESS_BLOCKED`, the **eighteenth consecutive run** confirming the restriction. No backlog upgrade was possible; three more cases were coded via WebSearch-only synthesis (items below), chosen for pathway/geography/origin variation per the standing instruction. **Progressive Art Collection** gives the corporate sub-population its first case built around a named, deceased individual founder whose collection then survived both his own death (2013) and his hired curator's death (2022) as an ordinary corporate program — a fifth, distinct governance-continuity mechanism (absorption into a going concern's operating budget) alongside the four the dataset had already surfaced. **Museo Jumex** is Latin America's second standalone-museum case after [[cisneros]] (a dispersal case, not a built institution) — a living, still-presiding founder, essentially no locatable financial disclosure of any kind, and a genuinely thin evidence base worth naming honestly rather than papering over. **Brauer Museum of Art / Valparaiso University** was explicitly flagged as a future-run candidate in [[market-intelligence]] #6's 2026-08-21 addendum — a university deaccessioning its own collection over the litigated, ultimately unsuccessful objection of the museum's own founding director, the sample's clearest instance yet of a parent institution overriding the person who actually built a collection. This reaches **70 of ~75 (near-term target; ~100 eventually) coded** (67 file-verified prior + 3 new; see the header correction note above).

### 69. Progressive Art Collection — Mayfield Village, OH · Spot-verified · full note [[progressive-art-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **hybrid** (an ongoing corporate workplace-display program; no template value cleanly fits "a company keeps buying and showing art indefinitely," a schema gap flagged alongside [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]]) · founder **Peter B. Lewis** deceased (d.2013) · **survived_founder yes** (13 yrs post-death, unaffected by either the founder's 2013 death or founding curator Toby Devan Lewis's own 2022 death) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **founder-sole → parent-institution** (always a corporate asset of the publicly traded Progressive Corporation, never a separate foundation) · **existing-institution** (distributed across ~400 offices, primary display at the Mayfield Village HQ) · coherence **broad-survey** (2,000+ artists, 70 countries, deliberately non-thesis) · decision **collector-alone → art-advisor → corporate-executive/staff**, three regimes in one case · **friction none-documented** · constraints **no**.
Numbers: collection size contested **30 works (1974 founding) → 6,000-6,500 (per 2013 obituary coverage) vs. 10,000+ (per a 2026 overview source)**, not reconciled, likely different count-dates across a 13-year gap. net_assets/expenses **n/a** — a public company's art program, not a distinct 990- or SEC-disaggregated reporting entity; a structurally distinct fourth kind of "no comparable disclosure," alongside the sample's IRS-990, Swiss-supervisory, and state-appropriations regimes. attendance/FTE **unknown**.
Sources: crainscleveland.com · progressive.com/artcollection · myartbroker.com · forbes.com (Lewis obituary) · artnews.com (Toby Devan Lewis obituary) · observer.case.edu

### 70. Museo Jumex / Fundación Jumex Arte Contemporánea — Mexico City, Mexico · Spot-verified · full note [[museo-jumex]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** (a 2001-2013 appointment-access gallery phase inside the family's own juice factory, then a purpose-built David Chipperfield museum) · founder **Eugenio López Alonso** living, still presiding · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **founder-open** · durability **moderate** (13 years continuous operation; no endowment or independent-board structure located) · governance **founder-sole** (no independent board surfaced distinct from López's own presidency of the Fundación) · **purpose-built** (2013, Polanco) · coherence **coherent-multi** (international + Latin American contemporary) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open** (living founder, no located succession statement).
Numbers: **no financial figures of any kind located** — net assets, expenses, endowment, collection size, and attendance are all `unknown`; Mexico has no US-990-equivalent public disclosure regime located this run, a probable fifth structurally-absent-disclosure jurisdiction alongside Kazakhstan, Thailand and Brazil, not yet added to that formal count pending explicit confirmation.
Sources: fundacionjumex.org (search-summary, not directly fetched) · en.wikipedia.org (Colección Jumex; Eugenio López Rodea) · wonderfulmuseums.com

### 71. Brauer Museum of Art — Valparaiso University, IN · Spot-verified · full note [[brauer-museum-valparaiso]]
pathway **hybrid** (+sell-private/sell-auction branch for the three deaccessioned works) · founder **n/a** (an institutional teaching collection, named for founding *director* Richard Brauer, not a wealth-source founder — the same schema gap [[cam-raleigh]]/[[michener-art-museum]] establish) · **survived_founder n/a** · outcome **distressed** · durability **weak** (director role + 13 staff positions eliminated June 2024; museum closed, reopened Nov 2024 at ~20 hrs/week) · governance **parent-institution** (Valparaiso University board of trustees) · **purpose-built** (no building sale — the collection, not the real estate, was the liquidated asset, the inverse of [[cam-raleigh]]/[[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]]) · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **[schema gap — no `decision_owner` value fits "a parent institution overriding its own museum's founding director"; coded as the closest available, foundation-executive, flagged inexact]** · **friction funding-gap** (+governance-conflict, legal-challenge) · constraints **yes** (asserted and litigated, though the 2023 lawsuit was dismissed on standing, not decided on the restriction's merits).
Numbers: three works (Church, Hassam, O'Keeffe) collectively estimated **~$20M** at the 2023 proposal (O'Keeffe alone ~$15M); a later, separate ~**$12M** estimate for the Church sale specifically, unreconciled against the original three-work figure. **Confirmed final sale prices for the Hassam and O'Keeffe were not located this run**, despite press describing both as "finalized"/"offloaded" — a specific, real gap, not a placeholder. Founding director Richard Brauer, 97 as of the litigation's final stage, was reported living and personally contesting the sale before dropping the suit in Aug 2024 to avoid further legal-fee risk.
Sources: theartnewspaper.com (2023-04-25, 2024-06-28, 2024-08-30) · artnews.com · en.wikipedia.org (Valparaiso University deaccessioning controversy) · chicagobusiness.com

**New pattern this batch: a fifth governance-continuity mechanism — absorption into an ordinary going concern.** The dataset's survivors so far persist through four identified mechanisms: a self-perpetuating independent board (MoMA, Menil), a locked family succession (Hillwood), a permanence doctrine that pre-decides discretionary questions (Chinati), or a forced governance handoff during a founder-sole crisis while the founder still lived (Dia, Inhotim). [[progressive-art-collection]] adds a fifth: the collection was never structurally dependent on any one person's survival at all, because it was always an ordinary line item inside a large, stable corporation — it outlived its individual founder (2013) and its founding curator (2022) not because either contingency was specifically planned for, but because neither was ever a precondition for the program's continuation. This is a different *kind* of durability than the case-template's founder-centric governance vocabulary was built to describe, and worth naming as its own category rather than forcing it into `founder-sole`/`independent-board`.

**Original cross-case analysis this batch: a full pathway × outcome pull across all 67 file-verified prior-coded cases, run specifically to try to break H3 (Part D).** See [[what-we-now-believe]] for the full write-up. In short: built-institution pathways (found-standalone-museum, found-house-museum, found-art-park; n=25 definitive-outcome cases, excluding six too-new-to-judge `emerging`/`pivoted` cases) had only **2 of 25 failures**, against **1 of 10** for lighter models (long-term-loan, traveling-program, intentional-dispersal) — raw counts only, no rate or statistical-equivalence claim published off this hand-selected set (QA finding #1, 2026-08-22) — directly meeting H3's own stated disconfirmation target ("standalone museums surviving at the same rate as lighter models"). The finding is reported with a specific, honest caveat: a hand-picked sample built substantially via WebSearch likely underrepresents standalone museums that failed and disappeared decades ago (they leave little current web footprint to be found by), so the true gap may be larger than this count shows — narrowed confidence, not a reversal.

Four new cases, each now also coded on the five governance sub-fields (`board_type` · `succession_locked_before_founder_death` · `founder_control_mechanism` · `donor_intent_instrument` · `endowment_governance`). **Process note: this run's WebFetch tool was blocked by network egress policy on every primary domain attempted** (ProPublica Nonprofit Explorer, institution sites, The Art Newspaper, Artnet, ARTnews, Sotheby's, Wikipedia). All four cases were researched via WebSearch snippet synthesis instead of direct primary reads, so every figure below — including 990/990-PF numbers that would normally be `primary` — is tagged `secondary` in the source case files. Numbers: 2010 lifetime gift of **367–419 works** to the Whitney (~$50–75M) [Whitney, secondary]; 2023 Sotheby's estate sale **$424.7M** across ~111 lots — **5–8x the dollar value of the Whitney gift from a fraction of the piece count** [Sotheby's/Artnet, secondary]. **Largest unresolved gap in this batch:** the 2010 gift + 2023 sale together account for at most ~530 works against a reported 1,200–1,500-work lifetime collection; the fate of the remaining ~700–1,000 works is undocumented in any source found. Foundation net assets swung $9.9M (FY2022) → $2.86M (FY2024) against $6.78M in FY2024 disbursements — a pattern *consistent with* (not confirmed as) a pass-through of part of the auction proceeds.
Sources: sothebys.com/en/articles/historic-auctions-see-emily-fisher-landau-collection-reach-425-million · theartnewspaper.com (2023 obituary) · news.artnet.com (2023 sale results) · causeiq.com EIN 13-7325028

**New pattern this batch: a third and fourth failure mode.** The original pilot found two clocks — governance failing fast at death (Terra), funding failing slow over decades (Corcoran). This batch adds two more shapes, both worth testing at scale rather than trusting from n=1 each: **(3) founder-controlled closure while still living, with disposition then left to an estate process** (Fisher Landau — closed 2017, six years pre-death, no locked plan found for the remaining collection), and **(4) a labor/governance rupture with no funding or succession problem at all** (Marciano — foundation held ~$50M, both founders alive, and the public program still went dark for over four years). Both sit outside the founder-status-at-death framing the `survived_founder` field was built around, which is itself a finding about the field's limits worth flagging before the 50-case pass locks the schema.

### 16. Lehman Brothers Art Collection — New York, NY · Spot-verified · full note [[lehman-brothers-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **sell-auction** (full liquidation) · **founder n/a — corporate collection**, originally assembled for Neuberger Berman by curator I. Michael Danoff, acquired by Lehman with the 2003 Neuberger Berman purchase · outcome **dissolved** · durability **failed** (100% liquidated) · governance **n/a** (bankruptcy estate/creditors' committee, not a museum board — the template has **no field for this decision-maker type**, a schema-coverage gap worth flagging for any future corporate case) · **no-building** · coherence broad-survey · decision **auction-house** (execution) / bankruptcy process (actual decision) · **friction funding-gap**, in the specific form of the parent company's 2008 Chapter 11 filing, the largest U.S. bankruptcy in history.
Numbers: Sotheby's New York, Sept 25 2010: **$12.3M** (118 works) [NPR, secondary]. Christie's London, Sept 29 2010: **≈$2.57M** [Art Observed, secondary]. Freeman's Philadelphia, Nov 7 2010: smallest sale, total **not isolated — unknown**. Aggregate widely reported as **"~$16M"**, which does not fully reconcile against the ~$14.9M the two confirmed sales sum to — flagged as an open, unreconciled contradiction rather than silently resolved.
Sources: npr.org/2010/09/24/130093669 · artobserved.com (Sotheby's and Christie's 2010 results) · pbs.org/newshour (Lehman art coverage)

**New pattern this batch: succession can be architectural, not just legal or governance-structural.** Kreeger is the first case in the sample where the succession mechanism is physically built into the institution — a house purpose-designed decades before the founder's death to convert into a museum — distinct from the governance-instrument mechanisms (wills, foundation charters, board continuity) that carry the survival stories so far. It raises a question for the 50-case pass: how many other house-museums (McNay, Phillips, Weisman) had this kind of physical pre-commitment vs. a purely legal one, and does the architectural version correlate with anything different in outcome?

**New pattern this batch: two schema-coverage gaps, both worth naming before the sample locks.** Michener (civic/government founding, no identifiable collector-decider) and Lehman (corporate bankruptcy-estate decision-maker) both resist the current `decision_owner` and governance-sub-field vocabulary, which assumes a person, family, or museum/foundation board sits behind every transition. Two cases is not yet a pattern to size, but it is now a confirmed *type* of gap — collector-centric fields undercounting civic and corporate origination stories — that the report should either name as an explicit limitation or extend the schema to cover before publishing rates.

## Batch 4 (2026-08-14, second run) — a second corporate bankruptcy, a corporate voluntary pivot, an art-park gap-filler, and the first international case

Four new cases (37th–40th coded overall, numbered 17–20 in this dataset's own sequence). **Process note: WebFetch was re-tested this run against the widest domain sweep yet** — projects.propublica.org, www.sidrichardsonmuseum.org, and en.wikipedia.org all returned `EGRESS_BLOCKED` — confirming this is a blanket session-level network-egress restriction, not a ProPublica-specific or museum-specific block. Per the standing instruction, no primary-verification upgrade was possible this run (step 2 could not proceed); instead this run went straight to step 3, coding four new cases via WebSearch snippet synthesis, tagged `secondary` throughout, and adding all four to the Primary-verification backlog above. Cases were chosen deliberately to fill gaps the pattern-tracking below had already flagged: a second corporate-failure data point (to test whether Lehman's clock generalizes), a corporate case where the *parent is healthy* (a new mechanism, distinct from bankruptcy), an art-park case where the *original site* didn't continue (the pathway previously had zero failure examples), and the sample's first fully international, non-US/non-European-benchmark case.

### 17. Enron Art Collection — Houston, TX · Spot-verified · full note [[enron-art-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **sell-auction** · **founder n/a — corporate collection**, assembled under Lee Fastow's direction only a few years before the 2001 collapse · outcome **dissolved** · durability **failed** (100% liquidated) · governance **n/a** (bankruptcy estate/creditors' process; same schema gap as Lehman) · **no-building** · coherence broad-survey · decision **auction-house** (execution) / bankruptcy process (actual decision) · **friction funding-gap** (Dec 2001 Chapter 11, one of the largest U.S. bankruptcies at the time).
Numbers: one 2002 sale of 5 works realized under $1M combined, including a $405,500 sale (with commission) against a $574,500 purchase price two years earlier — a documented loss [UPI, WaPo, secondary]. A separate reported total of "$1.74M" (artdaily headline) does not clearly reconcile against the ~$1M figure — flagged open, not resolved. No comprehensive across-all-sales total found.
Sources: upi.com/Odd_News/2002/02/06/Enron-Debacle-Hits-Art-World-Hard · washingtonpost.com/archive/lifestyle/2003/05/16/the-gavel-falls-on-enron-artworks · artdaily.cc (title only, not independently fetched)

### 18. Deutsche Bank Collection / PalaisPopulaire — Frankfurt / Berlin, DE · Spot-verified · full note [[deutsche-bank-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
**Re-researched and upgraded to full [[case-template]] compliance 2026-08-22** (public-projection layer added; closure mechanism, founding history, and governance sharpened against fresh search results — see the file for the complete rewrite). pathway **hybrid** (leased venue 2018–2026 → rotating-loan/traveling model 2027+) · **founder n/a — corporate collection**, first purchases ~1979 (Herbert Zapp, 57 Beuys drawings), "official" bank-stated founding 1980, anchored to the 150th anniversary of Deutsche Bank's own 1870 founding — a milder, anniversary-driven instance of the corporate founding-date-compression pattern, distinct from UBS's merger-driven version · outcome **pivoted** (live, in-progress; closure completes end of 2026) · durability **moderate** (institutional-visibility contraction; underlying collection explicitly not dispersed) · governance **parent-institution** (Deutsche Bank AG's internal Art & Culture division; named leadership lineage Zapp → Friedhelm Hütte, 1986–2022 → Britta Färber, 2022–present — unusually well-documented for a corporate case, though budget/headcount remain fully undisclosed) · **existing-institution → no-building** (PalaisPopulaire was **leased, not owned** — from Mathias Döpfner's Axel Springer — and the closure follows a lease non-renewal, not a sale) · coherence **coherent-multi** (a stated works-on-paper/photography, post-1945 thesis inside a large multi-artist holding) · decision **no-identifiable-person** (schema gap; role-level leadership named, decision-level owner not) · **friction funding-gap**, specifically a landlord lease non-renewal, in a strategic rather than distress sense.
Numbers: collection size **contested, not reconciled** — ~50,000 (older) / "over 55,000" / "over 57,000" (current) works [db.com, Frieze, secondary]; distribution footprint also contested (500 vs. ~900 locations). A 2020 precedent sold ~200 modern works to fund new acquisitions [Art Newspaper 2020, secondary]. No art-program-specific budget/financial figures found — confirmed structural, not merely unpulled (German public company, no US-990 equivalent); the collection is confirmed **not** held by the separate Deutsche Bank Stiftung.
Sources: kunstforum.de/en/news/deutsche-banks-palaispopulaire-exhibition-centre-is-closing · tagesspiegel.de (lease/closure detail) · frieze.com/article/delving-deutsche-bank-collection · art.db.com/deutsche-bank-collection · theartnewspaper.com/2020/02/04/goodbye-gerhard-deutsche-bank-shrinks-art-collection · db.com news release 2025-11-19 (Artist of the Year 2026) · handelsblatt.com / monopol-magazin.de (Hütte obituary) · de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Bank_Stiftung

### 19. Benini Sculpture Ranch / MuseoBenini — Johnson City → Marble Falls, TX · Spot-verified · full note [[benini]]
pathway **hybrid** (found-art-park 1999–2014 → found-house-museum/single-artist museum 2015–present) · founders **Benini & Lorraine Benini** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (both founders alive) · outcome **pivoted** · durability **moderate** (new 501(c)(3) only granted June 2025) · governance **founder-sole → mixed** (Benini now honorary board chair of a newly formalized foundation) · **no-building → purpose-built** (MuseoBenini, 2014–15) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (single-artist museum) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction unknown** (no source states why the original 140-acre ranch ended) · constraints **open** (founders living).
Numbers: original ranch 1999–2014, 140 acres, reported 2,000–3,000 visitors/month; MuseoBenini opened 2015, 6,500 sq ft; Benini Foundation granted 501(c)(3) June 2025. All financials unknown — the foundation is too newly recognized to have a filed Form 990 discoverable this session, a genuinely *new* unknown (not-yet-created), distinct from most backlog cases' *not-yet-pulled* unknowns.
Sources: rockoakdeer.blogspot.com/2012/06/benini-sculpture-ranch.html · glasstire.com/2020/08/15/a-visit-to-museobenini-in-marble-falls · museobenini.com/benini-press-release-fall-2025

### 20. Yuz Museum Shanghai / Yuz Foundation — Shanghai, China · Spot-verified · full note [[yuz-museum]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** (+ attempted, uncompleted LACMA joint-foundation branch) · founder **Budi Tek** deceased (d. March 2022, age 65) · **survived_founder not-yet-testable, trending yes** (~4.4 yrs since death, one director succession, new venue built 2023, continued 2024–25 programming) · outcome **stable** · durability **indeterminate** (no financial data of any kind — China has no 990-equivalent disclosure regime, a systematically different `unknown` than the sample's other cases) · governance **founder-sole → family** (daughter Justine Alexandria Tek now Director) · **purpose-built** (new venue, 2023) · coherence coherent-multi · decision **collector-alone → family** · **friction succession-failure, but attempted-and-blocked** — Tek pursued a joint foundation with LACMA from 2018 to lock succession before death, undone by geopolitics and the pandemic rather than abandoned or contested · constraints partial.
Numbers: institution opened 2014 [not independently re-confirmed this session]; collection ~1,500 works [secondary, treat cautiously — an adjacent "50 years" collecting-span figure found in the same search synthesis is very likely erroneous and was not coded]. All financial fields unknown.
Sources: news.artnet.com/art-world/budi-tek-obituary-2088191 · unframed.lacma.org/2022/03/21/memoriam-budi-tek-1957%E2%80%932022 · news.artnet.com/art-world/justine-alexandria-tek-interview-yuz-museum-budi-tek-2245866

**New pattern this batch: the corporate-failure clock generalizes, but severity varies with what was bought and when.** Enron is the sample's second corporate-bankruptcy dispersal (after Lehman) and confirms the basic mechanism — a parent's balance-sheet collapse liquidates an unrelated art asset regardless of the collection's own merits — but adds a variable Lehman's case didn't show: **timing and cost basis at forced sale.** Lehman's Sotheby's lot sold *above* pre-sale estimate in 2010; Enron's flagship lot sold for a documented ~30% loss against its own purchase price in 2002, in a market shaped by the seller's own scandal. Two cases is still not a rate, but it now separates "did the parent fail" from "how much value survived the forced sale" as a second, independent axis worth tracking as more corporate cases are coded.

**New pattern this batch: a corporate collection can retreat from public visibility without any financial distress at all — a fourth failure-adjacent mode, but not a failure.** Deutsche Bank's PalaisPopulaire closure is the first corporate case in the sample where the parent is financially healthy and the collection is explicitly *not* dispersed — only the dedicated public venue is. This is mechanistically the same "building stopped paying for itself, mission/collection continues" pattern the report already logs for CAM Raleigh and the Rauschenberg Foundation HQ ([[candidate-universe]] Signals rows), now confirmed at large corporate scale. It sharpens rather than contradicts pattern 2 below (two failure clocks): this is neither clock, a reminder that `outcome_category: pivoted` needs to stay analytically distinct from `distressed`/`dissolved` even when the surface story ("closing a space") sounds similar.

**New pattern this batch: three distinct shapes of "unknown," worth naming explicitly before the 50-case pass locks the schema.** This batch's cases surface unknowns that are not interchangeable: (a) **unmeasurable in principle** — Yuz Museum's finances, where no 990-equivalent disclosure regime exists in China at all; (b) **not yet created** — Benini's Form 990, where the foundation was only granted 501(c)(3) status in June 2025 and a first filing likely doesn't exist yet; (c) **blocked by this session's tooling** — the great majority of the backlog, where a primary document (990, sale results, institution page) exists and would resolve the figure, but WebFetch could not reach it this run. The report's existing data-availability finding (pattern 4, below) currently treats these as one undifferentiated category; splitting them out is more honest and more useful to a reader trying to judge whether a gap is fixable.

**New pattern this batch: an attempted-but-uncompleted succession lock is a distinct governance outcome, not a subtype of "no plan."** Yuz Museum is the first case where a founder actively pursued a formal institutional succession mechanism (the LACMA joint foundation) years before death and had it blocked by forces outside his control (geopolitics, pandemic timing), yet the institution still continued via informal family succession. This sits on a spectrum with Kreeger (mechanism completed, just architectural rather than legal) and Terra (no mechanism ever attempted) — the `succession_locked_before_founder_death` field currently reads as binary yes/no/unknown, but "attempted and blocked" is analytically different from both an unattempted plan and a simple unknown, and the report should consider a fourth coded value once more international cases test whether this is common.

**Art-park pathway gap closed.** Before this batch, every art-park case in the sample (Chinati, Pyramid Hill, Longleaf) was a survival or successful-pivot story. Benini is the first art-park case where the *original site* did not continue — filling a real gap in the outcomes-by-pathway chart — though it is a uniquely low-stakes example of discontinuity: founder-driven, while both founders remain alive, with a successor institution already built and now formally incorporated. It cannot yet answer whether art parks fail differently than built museums; it only proves the pathway can end without death, litigation, or documented funding collapse.

## Batch 5 (2026-08-15, third run) — a 28-years-pre-death governance lock, a zero-endowment dispersal, a same-week spousal liquidation, and the first crisis-free corporate case

Four new cases (41st–44th coded overall, numbered 21–24 in this dataset's own sequence). **Process note: WebFetch was re-tested this run** against the widest sweep attempted yet — projects.propublica.org, www.sidrichardsonmuseum.org, and, new this run, a directly-linked third-party 990-PF PDF host (www.foundationsearch.com) surfaced by a WebSearch result, specifically to test whether a non-aggregator document host might sit outside the block. **All three returned `EGRESS_BLOCKED`** — the fourth consecutive run confirming a blanket session-level network-egress restriction, not a domain- or content-type-specific one. Per the standing instruction, no primary-verification upgrade was possible this run; instead this run coded four new cases via WebSearch snippet synthesis, tagged `secondary` throughout, and added all four to the Primary-verification backlog above. Cases were chosen to fill gaps the pattern-tracking had already flagged: a second, cleaner international deceased-founder-thriving benchmark (Yuz Museum is `stable`/financially `indeterminate`; this sample needed a case that is unambiguously thriving); the sample's most extreme wealth-scale outlier (to test "governance beats endowment" against a $0-endowment case); a fresh, near-contemporary post-death auction with a spousal decision-maker, for direct contrast against both Terra's litigated collapse and [[fisher-landau]]'s founder-controlled pre-death closure; and the sample's first corporate case defined by the *absence* of any crisis, for direct contrast against Lehman/Enron (bankruptcy) and Deutsche Bank (voluntary retreat).

### 21. Fondation Beyeler — Riehen/Basel, Switzerland · Spot-verified · full note [[fondation-beyeler]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founders **Ernst** (d. 2010) & **Hildy Beyeler** (d. 2008) deceased · **survived_founder yes** (16 yrs since Ernst's death, thriving and actively expanding) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** (qualitative) · governance **founder-sole → independent-board** (chaired by Hansjörg Wyss, no Beyeler family — Ernst Beyeler had no children) · **purpose-built** (Renzo Piano, 1997; Zumthor extension opening in phases 2026–27) · coherence coherent-multi (classical modernism) · decision collector-alone (founding) → foundation-executive (post-death) · constraints no.
Numbers: foundation established **1982** — 28 years before Ernst's death, 15 years before the museum opened — the sample's longest pre-death governance lock-in found to date; ~400 works; 9-millionth visitor reached 2024; ~10% of budget from Basel/Riehen, remainder mostly ticket sales/commercial; ~$18.7M reported 2026 revenue [low-confidence, single aggregator source]. true_endowment/net_assets/FTE **unknown** — no Swiss-foundation balance sheet located; a US-side "American Friends of Fondation Beyeler" 990 (EIN 56-2396274) is a real primary-source lead for a future run.
Sources: en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyeler_Foundation · en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Beyeler · archdaily.com/1042400 (Zumthor extension) · theartnewspaper.com/2017/06/15 (funding structure)

### 22. Dorothy and Herbert Vogel Collection — New York, NY → all 50 states · Spot-verified · full note [[vogel-collection]]
pathway **intentional-dispersal** (+donate-existing-museum-scattered branch, 1992 NGA gift) · founders **Herbert** (d. 2012) & **Dorothy Vogel** (d. 2025-11-10) deceased · **survived_founder yes**, distinctively **pre-executed** — the defining 50-states placements (2008–09) completed 3 years before Herbert's death and 16 years before Dorothy's, so there was nothing left to "survive" in the usual sense by the time either died · outcome **thriving** (as distributed) · durability **strong** (by construction — works landed inside 51 pre-existing, independently-resourced institutions, not dependent on any single entity) · governance founder-sole · **no-building** (never had a dedicated venue) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (minimalist/conceptual, 1960s–70s) · decision collector-alone · constraints partial (self-imposed one-work-per-state distribution rule).
Numbers: **$0 founding endowment, ever** — funded entirely from Herbert's postal-clerk salary, the couple living on Dorothy's librarian salary instead, the sample's only zero-capital case. ~1,100 works to the National Gallery (1992) + 2,500 works/177 artists to 50 states (2008–09); lifetime total reported "4,000+"/"5,000 works" — **the tranches don't reconcile against the lifetime total, a several-hundred-to-1,000+-work gap unresolved.** No operating entity to code net_assets/FTE/attendance — coded n/a, not unknown, since no such entity exists.
Sources: washingtonpost.com (Herbert 2012 obituary; Dorothy 2025 obituary) · nga.gov/stories/articles/remembering-vogels-modern-art-collectors · arts.gov Vogel program page · nga.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/nga-archives-vogel.pdf

### 23. De la Cruz Collection — Miami, FL · Spot-verified · full note [[de-la-cruz-collection]]
pathway **sell-auction** (+found-standalone-museum branch, 2009–2024) · founder **Rosa de la Cruz** deceased (d. 2024-02-25); husband/co-founder **Carlos de la Cruz** living · **survived_founder NO** — museum closed the same week as Rosa's death (she died on the museum's usual closed day; Carlos never reopened it) and never resumed, the fastest, least-contested closure in the sample · outcome **closed-dispersed** · durability **failed** · governance **founder-sole**, privately held (no independent board or 501(c)(3) structure identified) · **purpose-built** (2009, Design District) · coherence coherent-multi (contemporary; depth in Mendieta, González-Torres) · decision **spouse** (Carlos, immediate discretionary liquidation, no institutional intermediary) · **friction none-documented** as cause; the *auction decision itself* was publicly controversial in Miami's art community · constraints no (no will/instrument found; Carlos's own account suggests no pre-existing plan).
Numbers: first Christie's sale, May 2024: **26 lots, $34.4M**, against a reported pre-sale full-collection estimate of only **~$30M** — the opening sale alone already exceeded the total estimate, unreconciled. ~1,000-work lifetime collection, 200+ works consigned to Christie's. Building separately sold to ICA Miami for **$25M** (Oct 2024) — a distinct transaction from the art sale. No nonprofit balance sheet — private operation, `n/a` throughout for financial fields.
Sources: wlrn.org/arts-culture/2024-02-26 (Rosa obituary) · news.artnet.com/market/de-la-cruz-collection-sale-2457385 · artnews.com/art-news/news/ica-miami-buys-former-de-la-cruz-collection-building-1234721193

### 24. JPMorgan Chase Art Collection — New York, NY / global · Spot-verified · full note [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]] · `origin: corporate`
pathway **hybrid** (in-house corporate display + traveling-loan program) · **founder n/a — corporate collection**, founded 1959 by David Rockefeller as Chase Manhattan Bank president · outcome **stable** · durability **indeterminate** (no separable balance sheet) · governance **parent-institution**, same decision_owner schema gap as Lehman/Enron/Deutsche Bank — now 5 corporate cases hitting it · **existing-institution** (~450 corporate offices) · coherence broad-survey · decision **no-identifiable-person** (best fit; named director role exists — Lisa K. Erf) · **friction none-documented**.
Numbers: **30,000+ objects**; **survived at least 4 parent-company M&A events** (Chase–Chemical 1996, Chase–J.P.Morgan 2000, Bank One 2004, Bear Stearns/WaMu 2008) with no reported interruption — the sample's first evidence that M&A alone, as distinct from parent bankruptcy, does not disrupt a corporate collection. Traveling exhibition *Collected Visions* (~70 works) toured internationally (Pera Museum Istanbul, Bronx Museum). **Flag: [[candidate-universe]]'s "began selling parts in the 2020s" claim could NOT be corroborated this run** — no auction record, press report, or company statement found; treated the same as the unconfirmed First Republic Bank Collection claim, not coded as fact. No art-program-specific budget found — company financials not used as a proxy.
Sources: jpmorganchase.com/about/art-collection · bronxmuseum.org/exhibition/collected-visions · peramuseum.org/exhibition/collected-visions/113 · digitalcommons.rockefeller.edu/the-rockefellers/9 (1959 founding, via search synthesis)

**New pattern this batch: pre-death governance lock-in can predate the institution's own building by over a decade, and can bypass family succession entirely.** Fondation Beyeler's 1982 foundation charter — 15 years before the museum opened, 28 years before Ernst Beyeler's death — is the sample's earliest and most complete lock-in found, and unlike every other thriving deceased-founder survivor in the sample (de Menils, Phillipses, Havemeyer Webb), it handed to a wholly independent board with no family option available at all (Beyeler had no children). This strengthens pattern 1's core claim while showing it doesn't depend on family continuity as a mechanism.

**New pattern this batch: governance-over-endowment holds at the $0 extreme.** Every prior durability discussion in this dataset runs through some size of endowment, even a small or upper-bound one. [[vogel-collection]] has none at all, ever, and its dispersal-into-51-existing-institutions design reads as more durable by construction than cases with hundred-million-dollar endowments and single points of governance failure (Terra). This is the sharpest test yet of the report's central thesis, at a scale (postal-clerk salary) no other case in the sample approaches.

**New pattern this batch: the `spouse` decision_owner value hides two opposite outcomes.** [[weisman]]'s widow-successor and [[de-la-cruz-collection]]'s widower-liquidator are both coded `spouse`, but one institutionalizes continuity inside a standing foundation and the other executes immediate, unplanned dissolution. The field as currently specified can't distinguish these — worth flagging before `decision_owner` distributions are charted for the 50-case report.

**New pattern this batch: for corporate collections, M&A survival and bankruptcy survival are different tests, and the sample can now tell them apart.** [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]]'s 65-year, four-merger continuity, read against Lehman's and Enron's bankruptcy-triggered liquidations and Deutsche Bank's voluntary retreat, suggests corporate-collection mortality turns specifically on parent balance-sheet failure, not on ownership or strategy changes generally. Reframes pattern 2's corporate sub-typology as a three-way split (forced liquidation / voluntary retreat / unaffected continuity). The natural next test — an M&A absorption of a *distressed-but-not-bankrupt* parent — is Credit Suisse → UBS (2023), already identified in [[candidate-universe]] §8 and not yet coded.

**New pattern this batch: two more unconfirmed candidate-universe premises caught and flagged before they entered a coded case.** Following the same discipline applied to the First Republic Bank Collection claim (Batch 3), this run's research could not corroborate [[candidate-universe]]'s "JPMorgan began selling parts in the 2020s" claim despite a dedicated search pass. It is coded as an open flag in [[jpmorgan-chase-collection]], not as fact — a second instance of the credibility discipline (never assert a figure the research can't support) catching a plausible-sounding but unverified premise in the candidate pool before it reached a published case.

## Batch 6 (2026-08-15, fourth run) — reconciliation: 20 already-coded cases folded into the master dataset for the first time

**Process note.** This run began by re-testing WebFetch (blocked again, fifth consecutive run — see the Primary-verification backlog addendum above) and then discovered, while checking coverage against `ls cases/*.md` and `grep 'status: coded'`, that this file's own header and Patterns section had only ever synthesized **24 of the 44** files actually carrying `status: coded`. The other 20 — audain, barnes, bechtler, broad, chinati, cisneros, crystal-bridges, dali-museum, daskalopoulos, glenstone, grounds-for-sculpture, hillwood, kinsey, longleaf, magazzino, ogden, onera, pyramid-hill, storm-king, thoma — are genuine, sourced, `sample: report` case files (most with **primary** 990/990-PF figures pulled directly, from a session before this environment's WebFetch was blocked); they were simply never indexed into this "master" file's numbered list or counted in its coverage tally. Given this run's WebFetch was blocked again anyway, folding in 20 already-verified cases and re-deriving the patterns across the full 44 is a stronger use of the run than coding 2-4 more cases on top of a synthesis that was silently missing nearly half its own evidence base. No new primary research happened this run; every case below cites only what its own (pre-existing) file already established. Full coded headers, narratives, and gap lists live in each case's own file — entries below are compressed pointers, not replacements.

## Batch 20 (2026-08-22, twentieth run) — 75 of ~75: India's first case and a deferred-transfer university gift, plus a full re-research pass on an existing corporate case

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org — `EGRESS_BLOCKED`, the **twentieth consecutive confirmation**. Per the standing instruction, this run's Part A effort went to breadth: two new cases — [[jaipur-centre-for-art]] and [[hedreen-seattle-university]] — chosen for pathway/geography/origin variation (the sample's **first India case** and first royal-patronage-to-public origin type, a living head of a former princely family co-founding a professionally directed contemporary-art venue inside a still-occupied palace with no permanent collection at all; and the sample's third university-partnership case, and the first to code a **promised-but-not-yet-transferred** gift, with legal transfer of the art explicitly deferred until a not-yet-built museum is complete in 2028). Both received the full public-projection layer at coding time. A third file, [[deutsche-bank-collection]] (already coded, case #18), was independently re-researched and upgraded to full template compliance this run (public-projection layer added; the closure mechanism sharpened from a vague "strategic refocusing" to a specific lease non-renewal from a third-party landlord, governance/leadership lineage confirmed, and a milder, anniversary-driven instance of the founding-date-compression pattern documented) — see the revised case #18 entry above. Two of the five launch-set cases ([[cisneros]], [[fisher-sfmoma]]) also received their public-projection backfill this run, per the standing rotation (launch set first: [[cisneros]] ✓, [[fisher-sfmoma]] ✓, [[anderson-collection-stanford]], [[broad]], [[de-la-cruz-collection]] remain). **Coverage reaches 75 of ~75 near-term (77 case files carry `status: coded`, including [[deutsche-bank-collection]] which was already indexed as case #18 prior to this run's upgrade) — the near-term target Alina set 2026-08-18 is now met; per the standing "no hard cap" instruction, growth toward ~100 continues at the same 2-4-per-run pace rather than pausing.**

This run also carried a substantial QA-remediation pass (not part of Part A/B/C/D but recorded here for the audit trail): the 2026-08-22 QA audit's five CRITICAL findings were addressed where they touched files this routine owns — dropped an unpublishable rate off the hand-selected sample in both this file's own header text and the manuscript (raw counts substituted, no rate or statistical-equivalence claim), fixed a 2.4x market-size contradiction in [[transfer-scope-note]] against [[claims-register]] C6, softened an overstated "almost always unmanaged" claim in the manuscript to match H1's own restated wording, and corrected the manuscript's C10 wealth-manager restatement to match its already-correct framing elsewhere in the same file. A should-fix item (a stray `verification: press-sourced` value outside [[case-template]]'s controlled vocabulary) was also corrected on [[lucas-museum]], and a superseded 65%/2012 IRS Art Advisory Panel figure still stated as current in two research files was corrected to point to [[claims-register]] C31's 63%/FY2023 figure.

pathway **found-foundation** (a newly created, professionally directed exhibition/residency program; no permanent collection) · founders **Sawai Padmanabh Singh** (informal head, former Jaipur royal family) & **Noelle Kadar** (art advisor, co-founder/director), both **living** · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (institution under two years old, both founders active) · outcome **emerging** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **mixed** (family site/funding + outside professional co-director; no independent board identified) · **adapted-other** (a 2,600 sq ft wing of the still-occupied, multi-use City Palace complex) · coherence **coherent-multi** (a consistent curatorial thesis across changing exhibitions, not a fixed holding) · decision **art-advisor** (a genuine dual-founder case the single-value schema can't fully capture — see file) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open** (no governing instrument located; genuinely still-forming, not confirmed unconstrained).
Numbers: opened **22 Nov 2024**; no permanent collection (inaugural exhibition: 8 named artists incl. Anish Kapoor, Hiroshi Sugimoto, Dayanita Singh). All financial fields **unknown — structurally unresolvable, not merely unpulled**: India has no equivalent of the US Form 990, and it is not established this run whether JCA is a registered trust, society, Section 8 company, or an unincorporated family program — a jurisdiction-plus-entity-form gap distinct from a simple missing filing.
Sources: aninews.in (launch) · wallpaper.com · theartnewspaper.com/2025/02/03 · robbreportindia.com · culture360.asef.org · whitewall.art · museu.ms (the separate 1959 Museum Trust, not to be conflated with JCA)

### 75. The Hedreen Collection at Seattle University (SUMA) — Seattle, WA · Spot-verified · full note [[hedreen-seattle-university]]
pathway **university-partnership** (+found-standalone-museum) · founders **Richard C. Hedreen** (living, ~90–91) & **Betty Hedreen** (d. 2022, gift made in her honor) · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (institution does not yet exist) · outcome **emerging** (a funded, architected, dated plan under construction — distinct from open-ended `founder-open`) · durability **indeterminate** · governance **parent-institution** [inferred] (no separate incorporation announced; reported to Seattle University's Board of Trustees, the same structural pattern as [[anderson-collection-stanford]]) · **purpose-built** (Tom Kundig/Olson Kundig design; the existing Lee Center for the Arts is being demolished to site it) · coherence **coherent-multi** (Old Master through contemporary, "finely curated" per press, not a single tight thesis) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction community-opposition** (theater faculty/students/neighbors organized against the Lee Center demolition — a friction type not seen in [[ogden]] or [[anderson-collection-stanford]]) · constraints **open** (a living founder; the gift agreement is not public and legal transfer is explicitly deferred until the museum is complete).
Numbers: **200+ works, publicly valued ~$300M**, plus **$25M** construction seed funding (**~$325M** combined figure) [multiple press, secondary]. `institution_open_year`: **not yet — planned fall 2028**; the art has **not legally transferred** as of this coding, transfer explicitly conditioned on the building's completion — the sample's clearest promised-but-undelivered university gift. All financial fields **unknown — structural**: SUMA has no separate legal identity or Form 990 yet.
Sources: theartnewspaper.com/2024/03/15 ($325M framing) · archpaper.com/2025/04 (architect) · capitolhillseattle.com (2025-07, 2025-02 — Lee Center dispute) · seattlespectator.com (2025-04, 2026-02) · obituaries.seattletimes.com (Betty Hedreen)

**New pattern this batch: the university-partnership pathway now has a third, structurally distinct sub-form.** [[ogden]] folded a living founder's regional-canon collection into an *existing* university museum system; [[anderson-collection-stanford]] gave two now-deceased founders' collection its own purpose-built building, transferred at founding. [[hedreen-seattle-university]] resembles Anderson in shape (a free-standing purpose-built building on university land) but adds a feature neither prior case carries: the art has **not yet legally transferred**, with transfer explicitly gated on the building's completion years after the pledge was announced. Whether "pledge now, deliver on completion" is a genuine fourth sub-form or a timing artifact of catching this case mid-construction is the open question — but it is the sample's first coded instance of a founder retaining leverage over an announced gift via a deferred-transfer structure, a live H8 (founder-constraint design) data point distinct from every prior university case.

**New pattern this batch: a collecting institution can exist with no collection at all, and the absence is itself the finding.** [[jaipur-centre-for-art]] joins [[onera]] and [[tselinny-center]] as a third case testing this sample's own definition of its subject: a newly founded, professionally directed exhibition space that deliberately stages rotating shows and a residency/film program rather than assembling or displaying any permanent holding. Distinct from the other two, JCA adds a **dual-founder** structure the single-value `decision_owner` field cannot cleanly capture — a hereditary patron providing the site and (presumed) funding, paired with an independent art advisor as co-founder and operating director from day one. Read against H7A (the unowned pre-decision), this is a case where the advisor-coordination gap the hypothesis describes is closed *at the moment of founding* rather than left open — the opposite of most of this sample's cases, worth tracking as a possible model.

**New pattern this batch: India joins the sample's growing list of jurisdictions with no US-990-equivalent disclosure regime, but with its own three-way internal split.** Unlike a single missing-regime finding, [[jaipur-centre-for-art]]'s file documents that India actually has *three* distinct nonprofit filing regimes (state Charity Commissioner for Trusts, state Registrar of Societies, and the central Ministry of Corporate Affairs for Section 8 companies) — none centrally searchable the way ProPublica indexes US 990s — and that it is not established which, if any, JCA has adopted. This is the same "entity-form-unconfirmed," not just "jurisdiction-unconfirmed," gap [[khao-yai-art-forest]]'s Thailand file first raised, now confirmed on a second continent.

## Batch 21 (2026-08-23, twenty-first run) — a non-museum legacy vehicle and a founder who died mid-construction, plus a public-projection backfill on two corporate cases

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org — `EGRESS_BLOCKED`, the **twenty-first consecutive confirmation**. This run's session also opened on a detached-HEAD checkout of a commit that turned out to be disconnected from origin/main's actual current history (the remote had been rewritten upstream on a fresh 2026-08-20 "vault backup" root commit); the new history was confirmed to be a strict content superset of the old one (it adds `content/`, `export/`, `scripts/` on top of everything the old line had), so local `main` was realigned to `origin/main` before any of this run's own edits began — no work was lost or overwritten. Per the standing instruction, this run's Part A effort went to breadth: two new cases — [[klesch-collection-birkbeck]] and [[las-vegas-museum-of-art]] — chosen for pathway/geography/origin variation (a living UK-based collecting couple retaining their Old Master collection in full private ownership while funding a university AI/art-history postdoctoral fellowship instead of building or gifting to any institution, a genuinely new "keep the art, fund the scholarship" legacy shape; and a US founder who died in 2025, a year after founding her museum's current nonprofit and before any venue opened, with a documented **prior abandoned attempt** at the same underlying civic goal — an earlier 2010s effort folded into an existing Reno museum in 2018 rather than ever opening a Las Vegas venue). Both received the full public-projection layer at coding time. Two already-coded corporate cases also received their public-projection backfill this run, per the standing "onward" rotation past the now-complete five-case launch set: [[credit-suisse-ubs-collection]] (built from scratch — the case previously carried none of the flat `public_*` fields) and [[deutsche-bank-collection]] (converted from its prior prose "Public profile" body block, the deprecated pre-2026-08-22 format, to the current flat-frontmatter contract, and given its first `public_verified` value). `python scripts/export_public.py` was re-run after all four changes; the export grows from **9 to 13 collections**, privacy re-checked clean (no internal `outcome_category`/`verification`/`decision_owner`/`hypotheses`/`interview_status` fields leaked). **Coverage reaches 77 of ~75 (79 case files carry `status: coded`) — growth continues toward ~100 at the same 2-4-per-run pace, per the standing "no hard cap" instruction.**

Gary Klesch & Anita Klesch** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (no institution exists whose survival could be tested — the collection is retained, not transitioned) · outcome **founder-open** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole** · **no-building** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (15th–17th-century European Old Master painting) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: collecting began **2014** (a set of Arcimboldo's *Four Seasons*); **40+ paintings** [thekleschcollection.com, secondary]. Fellowship term **two years, 2026–2028**; funding amount **unknown** — not disclosed in any source found. All institutional financial fields **n/a**: no institution was founded; the fellowship is funded through Birkbeck, a public university whose consolidated accounts would not isolate a single named gift.
Sources: theartnewspaper.com/2026/08/20 · thekleschcollection.com/about · arthistorynews.com (the earlier, separate Klesch Collection Scholarship)

### 77. Las Vegas Museum of Art (LVMA) — Las Vegas, NV · Spot-verified · full note [[las-vegas-museum-of-art]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** (+abandoned-plan, +merger-into-institution, for a documented prior effort) · founder **Elaine Wynn** deceased (d. April 2025) · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** (no permanent venue open yet to test) · outcome **emerging** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **independent-board** (LACMA has stated LVMA operates independently, its own board/funding/leadership) · **purpose-built** (planned; interim venue **adapted-other**) · coherence **broad-survey** [inferred, via the LACMA partnership] · decision **collector-alone** · **friction construction-overrun** (opening slipped from fall 2028 to fall 2029) · constraints **no**.
Numbers: **legal_recognition_year 2024** [secondary, causeiq.com]; interim "Grand Central Galleries" opened **2026**; full museum planned **fall 2029**. Project cost **~$150M**, **60,000–90,000 sq ft**. A **50-year** LACMA partnership grants curatorial access to LACMA's ~150,000-object collection; LVMA holds no permanent collection of its own. All financial fields (net assets, expenses, FTE) **unknown** — WebFetch blocked, no primary 990 pull attempted; the institution may be young enough that no 990 is indexed yet.
Sources: theartnewspaper.com/2023/12/23 · reviewjournal.com/local · neon.reviewjournal.com · knpr.org/2018-06 (the 2018 merger predecessor) · fox5vegas.com/2025-04-23 (Wynn obituary/legacy) · en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaine_Wynn

**New pattern this batch: a collector's legacy vehicle can leave the collection itself completely untouched.** Every prior `found-*`/`retain-family` case in this sample assumes the collector's disposition decision is *about the collection*. [[klesch-collection-birkbeck]] breaks that assumption: the paintings stay in full private ownership, uncatalogued publicly, while a separate philanthropic act — funding a named university fellowship — carries the legacy weight instead. The current pathway vocabulary has no clean single term for this (coded here as `retain-family` + `secondary_pathways: [university-partnership]`, flagged in the case file itself as a schema-coverage note rather than an unauthorized new value); worth watching for a second instance before proposing a template amendment.

**New pattern this batch: a city can carry both a failed and a live attempt at the same civic goal, years apart, under unconnected sponsors.** [[las-vegas-museum-of-art]] is this sample's first case with a *documented, named prior failure of a structurally different vehicle* aimed at the identical underlying goal: a 2010s nonprofit ("Art Museum at Symphony Park") pursuing a Las Vegas art museum folded into the existing Nevada Museum of Art in 2018 rather than ever opening its own venue, before Elaine Wynn's entirely separate, unconnected 2024 nonprofit picked the same ambition back up. Every other `abandoned-plan` case in this dataset so far has been a dead end; this is the first coded instance of an abandoned plan and its live successor sitting in the same file.

## Batch 22 (2026-08-23, twenty-second run) — a landed-estate Kunsthalle, a digital-art institution, and a single-life "great estate rush" case

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org (re-test) and www.goodwood.com (fresh domain) — both `EGRESS_BLOCKED`, the **twenty-second consecutive confirmation**. Per the standing instruction, this run's Part A effort went to breadth: three new cases — [[goodwood-art-foundation]], [[0xcollection]], [[herbert-lust-collection]] — chosen for pathway/geography/origin variation (a UK landed-aristocratic estate spinning off a brand-new, non-collecting public charity while its own centuries-old family collection stays private and untouched, the sample's first Kunsthalle-shaped case; a Swiss digital/new-media art institution circulating commissioned work via touring partnerships rather than physical loans, the sample's first born-digital collecting focus and first case with an unresolved funder-vs-director decision-owner split; and a US lifetime museum donor whose estate remainder is now contested between two auction houses within months of his death, a single-life instance of the "great estate rush" thesis this dataset had previously only seen as a market-level pattern). All three received the full public-projection layer at coding time. **Public-projection field backfill is now complete across the entire coded set (80/80 coded files carry the flat `public_*` frontmatter)** — a milestone reached organically as this run's new cases closed the last gap; no backlog remains for future runs to work through, though `last_reviewed` refreshes and public-fact corrections should continue as cases are revisited. `python scripts/export_public.py` was re-run after coding; the export grows from **76 to 79 collections**. **Coverage reaches 80 of ~75 (a fresh `ls`/`grep` count this run found exactly 80 case files carry `status: coded`, reconciling exactly with the 80 numbered rows — see the integrity note above) — growth continues toward ~100 at the same 2-4-per-run pace, per the standing "no hard cap" instruction.**

### 80. Herbert Lust Collection — Greenwich, CT, USA (sale via New York) · Spot-verified · full note [[herbert-lust-collection]]
pathway **hybrid** (donate-existing-museum-scattered, lifetime → sell-auction, posthumous) · founder **Herbert Lust** deceased (d. May 12, 2026, age 99) · **survived_founder n/a** (personal collection, no institution founded) · outcome **closed-dispersed** · durability **n/a** · governance **founder-sole** · **no-building** · coherence **coherent-multi** [secondary] · decision **unknown** (estate executor not named in any source; the reported dynamic is auction-house-initiated courtship of the estate) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **unknown**.
Numbers: lifetime gifts of **200+ photographs** to the Hirshhorn Museum and **200+ Giacometti works** to the Tel Aviv Museum of Art [ARTnews, secondary]; estate remainder **~300 works, ~$60M estimated** [ARTnews, Robb Report, 2026, secondary], contested between Christie's and Sotheby's. Collecting-start year and lifetime total **unknown** ("thousands of works... the better part of a century"). All financial fields **n/a** — a personal collection with no institutional disclosure of any kind.
Sources: artnews.com/art-news/market/christies-sothebys-battle-herbert-lust-collection · robbreport.com · artnews.com (Lust obituary) · sothebys.com/en/articles/the-accidental-and-extraordinary-life-of-art-collector-herbert-lust

**New pattern this batch: a founder can build a public institution deliberately separate from their own inherited art.** Every prior `found-foundation` case in this sample (Chinati, Dia, Fondation Cartier, Fondazione Prada) puts the founder's own collection on public view. [[goodwood-art-foundation]] inverts that: a 350-year family collection stays private and off-site while the new public charity runs a pure rotating-commission program with no permanent collection at all — closer to a German-tradition Kunsthalle than to any other case in this dataset, and a genuine gap in `collection_coherence`/`collection_size_*`, both of which assume a permanent object collection exists.

**New pattern this batch: the medium itself, not just the access model, can be the novel thing an institution tests.** [[0xcollection]] extends this sample's "loans-only, no building" family (previously Onera, Thoma, Souls Grown Deep) into a collecting category — commissioned digital and AI-generated art — where the physical-object assumptions behind `collection_size_current` and even `decision_owner` (a funder and a director are explicitly different people here, with no source resolving which one made the founding pathway decision) stop applying cleanly. Coded `unknown` rather than guessed on both fields, a genuine structural gap rather than an unpulled figure.

**New pattern this batch: the "great estate rush" this dataset's market-intelligence has documented as an aggregate trend shows up, for the first time, inside a single coded life.** [[herbert-lust-collection]] ran both of this sample's disposal tracks himself, in sequence: donating hundreds of works to two museums on two continents during his lifetime, then leaving several hundred more for his estate to sell. The auction-house consignment competition that followed his death within months, with the advantage reportedly going to the house with the longer prior relationship rather than to any named independent coordinator, is this dataset's most direct, real-time illustration yet of the H7A "unowned pre-decision" finding — not inferred from a gap in the record, but observed happening.

**New pattern this batch: the same living founder can run two structurally distinct legacy pathways at once.** [[art-bridges-foundation]] is Alice Walton's second, later, and pathway-distinct vehicle alongside her own [[crystal-bridges]] standalone museum — a real-world instance of the "portfolio of dispositions" framing [[research-program]]'s Transfer inquiry area names, and this sample's first case of one founder appearing twice under two different coded pathways rather than once.

**New pattern this batch: a venue closure can be read at the wrong level if the collection itself is not distressed.** [[julia-stoschek-foundation]] closes a major public building from a position of reported strength — the same "voluntary optimization, not funding-gap" schema gap [[rauschenberg-foundation-hq]] first flagged — sharpening the case for a future `primary_friction` value distinguishing elective contraction from every distress-driven closure already in this sample (CAM Raleigh, Deutsche Bank/PalaisPopulaire, Rubin Museum).

**New pattern this batch: two UK charity cases, coded a run apart, land on opposite sides of the same disclosure question.** [[goodwood-art-foundation]] (Batch 22) could not confirm UK charitable status existed at all. [[amoca-cardiff]], coded this run, confirms its charity registration directly against the UK Charity Commission's own public register (number 1214619, dated 2025-08-20) — a genuine primary-registry hit obtained via WebSearch without a blocked fetch. The contrast is itself a finding: "no comparable disclosure regime" (this dataset's 13-case bucket) and "regime exists but nothing has been filed yet because the institution is too young" are different gaps, and only a name-by-name check, not an assumption from jurisdiction alone, tells them apart.

### 86. The Feuerle Collection — Berlin, Germany · Spot-verified · full note [[feuerle-collection]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founder **Désiré Feuerle** (with co-founder Sara Puig) living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **thriving** (10 continuous years open; durability coded `moderate`, not `strong`, since no financials were located to verify the label) · governance **founder-sole** — described on the venue's own site as a "non-profit organization" but **no independent board or governing document was located**, the same pattern [[sammlung-boros]] surfaced first, now a tracked two-case cluster · **adapted-other** (a pair of 1942-44 WWII telecommunications bunkers, converted by John Pawson, 6,350 sq m) · coherence **coherent-multi** (Imperial Chinese furniture + Khmer sculpture + contemporary art, deliberately juxtaposed across eras rather than organized around one thesis) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: opened **2016**; conversion cost explicitly marked **"Cost Undisclosed"** in the venue's own press materials [primary, for the fact of non-disclosure] · no German registry filing located this run — net assets/expenses/true endowment/FTE/attendance/collection size all **unknown**, a genuinely unlocated rather than merely unpulled set of gaps.
Sources: thefeuerlecollection.org/en/the-collection · dezeen.com/2016/11/23 · apollo-magazine.com/feuerle-collection-berlin

**New pattern this batch: `status: seed` can silently outlive its own promotion criteria.** [[lucas-museum]] had been named in this routine's own standing instructions as a public-field formatting exemplar for months while still carrying `status: seed` rather than `status: coded` — the exact "coded but not indexed" failure mode this file has caught twice before (Batch 6, Batch 19), this time running the other direction (indexed and cited, but never actually promoted). Worth a standing check: any file cited as an exemplar in the routine's own instructions should be verified against its own `status` field periodically, not assumed current because it is well-known.

**New pattern this batch: "990-PF filing status" and "founder-sole governance" are not the same claim, and this sample had been treating them as close proxies.** Every other 990-PF filer in this dataset (Glenstone, Magazzino, Thoma) is a tight family vehicle with no located independent board. [[lucas-museum]] breaks that correlation cleanly: a 990-PF filer with a board that includes a sitting rival-museum director and multiple non-family executives. The next full governance-table recompute (Pattern 14) should stop reading filing type as a governance-shape shortcut and check board composition case by case, as this dataset's own template already instructs but this batch is the first to actually contradict the correlation in practice.

**New pattern this batch: the "foundation/non-profit label without a locatable independent board" gap is now a confirmed two-case cluster, not a single observation.** [[sammlung-boros]] and [[feuerle-collection]] share the exact shape: an appointment-only European private museum, self-described in press or on its own site as a foundation or non-profit, with no independently locatable governing board or charter. Both remain coded `founder-sole` on an absence-of-evidence basis, the correct but weakest form of that field's evidence per [[case-template]]'s own gaps-are-a-finding principle. Worth a standing watch as more appointment-only European cases are added: if a third and fourth case share the same shape, it becomes a genuine finding about how this specific institutional type (private, appointment-only, converted-building, European) tends to self-describe versus how it is actually governed — distinct from, and worth contrasting against, this dataset's US pattern where 990-PF status at least confirms *some* filed governance structure exists, even when it is family-controlled.

### 96. Alfred Stieglitz Collection (Fisk University / Crystal Bridges) — Nashville, TN + Bentonville, AR, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[stieglitz-collection-fisk-crystal-bridges]]
pathway **hybrid** · `secondary_pathways` [donate-existing-museum-intact, sell-private, university-partnership, traveling-program] · founder **Georgia O'Keeffe** (donor) deceased (d. 1986) · **survived_founder yes** (in a materially altered legal form) · outcome **stable** · durability **moderate** · governance **parent-institution** (Fisk University's own board, not a founder-controlled entity) · **existing-institution** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** · decision **no-identifiable-person** (a university board acting under financial duress with state AG charitable-trust oversight — a fresh instance of Pattern 6's institutional-decision gap) · **friction funding-gap** · constraints **yes** (one of the sample's most explicit restrictions — "could neither be sold nor broken up" — and it did not hold literally).
Numbers: 101 works (97 from Stieglitz's estate, 4 O'Keeffe's own) [secondary, consistent across sources], unchanged since 1949; the one hard figure, Crystal Bridges paid **$30M** for its 50% interest (2012, court-approved). Financials: unknown (Fisk is a university, not a disaggregated art-specific filer — a structural, not tooling, gap).
**New pattern this case: a founder-imposed constraint's durability depends on whose solvency it is vested in, not just the founder's own governance design.** Every prior constraint-vs-survival case in the sample (Barnes, Chinati, Neue Galerie) involves a founder restricting an institution *they built and controlled*. Here O'Keeffe's restriction was vested in a *pre-existing, independently governed institution* she never controlled; when Fisk's own unrelated financial health came into conflict with the restriction, a Tennessee court applied the cy près doctrine (see [[market-intelligence]] #8 addendum, C66) to approve a modified outcome, sale of half the interest, not full compliance or a voided gift. This complicates H8's frame: a founder-controlled entity can hold a constraint until the founder's *own* governance fails it; a constraint vested in a third-party recipient is only as durable as that recipient's unrelated solvency, which no donor can design for. Also the sample's first HBCU case.
Sources: Crystal Bridges · ARTnews · Hyperallergic · NPR · AFRO American Newspapers.

### 97. The Rachofsky Collection / The Warehouse Dallas Art Foundation — Dallas, TX, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[rachofsky-warehouse-dallas]]
pathway **hybrid** · `secondary_pathways` [donate-existing-museum-intact, found-foundation] · founder **Cindy & Howard Rachofsky** (Warehouse: with Thomas Hartland-Mackie, 2024) living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **stable** · durability **moderate** · governance **mixed** (Warehouse co-founded by two collector families, not a sole founder) · **adapted-other** (converted industrial warehouse) · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **collector-alone** (nearest fit; the 2005 DMA pledge was actually a coordinated 5-family decision, a vocabulary gap flagged in the narrative) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **yes, but deliberately loose** — a "whatever remains at the second death" bequest formula, functionally the opposite of Barnes's or O'Keeffe's frozen-at-transfer restrictions.
Numbers: 900+ works pledged to DMA [secondary]; transition_year 2005 (pledge, not yet executed); institution_open_year 2012 (Warehouse) / 2024 (Foundation formalized). Financials unknown.
**New pattern this case: H8's loose-constraint extreme, and a distinct multi-family pledge shape.** Where Stieglitz/Fisk (above) shows a tight constraint failing once vested in someone else's solvency, Rachofsky shows the opposite design choice made deliberately by a living founder still in control: preserve maximum lifetime editing freedom, commit only "whatever's left." A second finding worth flagging: the 2005 pledge was a coordinated five-family agreement to one museum, not a single collector's decision or the Art Omi-style each-collector-builds-their-own-structure model — a third multi-party pledge shape for the taxonomy discussion. One open contradiction not resolved this run: a 2025 report has the Rachofsky House itself listed for sale, in apparent tension with 2005 reporting that the house was part of the DMA pledge; flagged, not asserted either way.
Sources: ARTnews · Glasstire · Dallas Morning News · Artnet News · The Warehouse Dallas · Artsy.

### 98. Société Générale Art Collection — Paris (La Défense) and Val-de-Fontenay, France · Spot-verified · full note [[societe-generale-collection]]
pathway **hybrid** · origin **corporate** · founder **n/a** (institutional decision under chairman Marc Viénot, 1995) · **survived_founder n/a** · outcome **stable** · durability **moderate** · governance **parent-institution** · **existing-institution** · coherence **broad-survey** · decision **no-identifiable-person** (per the 2026-08-25 QA-corrected vocabulary) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **n/a**.
Numbers: contested 1,200 vs. ~1,800 works [secondary, not reconciled]; collecting_start_year 1995. Financials n/a (a corporate operating asset; France's disclosure regime for a listed bank is consolidated IFRS/AMF financial statements, not an itemized art-asset filing — a fourth national corporate-disclosure shape after the US SEC-10-K non-disaggregation ([[progressive-art-collection]]), the Swiss foundation-supervisory regime, and Canada's CRA T3010).
**New pattern this case: the sample's first French corporate collection, and a structural split the corporate sub-sample hadn't shown yet.** Société Générale holds its art collection directly (no separate foundation entity), while running a legally and financially distinct philanthropic vehicle, the Société Générale Fondation, for grants — a split this sample hasn't had to code before, since [[ubs-art-collection]] and [[progressive-art-collection]] hold their collections with no parallel foundation to compare against, and [[fondation-cartier]]/[[fondazione-prada]] are themselves the foundation model rather than a split from one. Also a provisional sixth data point for Pattern 9 (corporate survival tracks parent balance-sheet health, not crisis exposure): no source located this run connects the bank's own well-documented 2008 Kerviel trading-loss scandal (€4.9B) to any interruption of the art program, though this is coded as an absence-of-evidence finding, not a confirmed "no impact."

pathway **sell-auction** · founder **Nelly Arrieta de Blaquier & Carlos Pedro Blaquier** both deceased (2020, 2023) · **survived_founder no** · outcome **closed-dispersed** · durability **failed** (as an intact collection) · governance **founder-sole** · **no-building** · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **children** [inferred — no individual heir named in sources] · **friction legal-challenge** (a 2023 Argentine customs export fine delayed the sale until its July 2026 waiver) · constraints **no**.

### 103. The Sandy Lerner Collection (cat-themed art and decorative arts) — Upperville, VA (collection) / New York, NY + MA (sale) · Spot-verified · full note [[sandy-lerner-cat-collection]]
pathway **sell-auction** · founder **Sandy Lerner** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **closed-dispersed** · durability **failed** (as an intact collection; a planned, founder-driven dispersal, not a distress sale) · governance **founder-sole** · **no-building** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (cat-related art and objects, four millennia) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **n/a**.
Numbers: nearly 1,000 lots across four Bonhams auctions, September–October 2026; collecting began circa 1990s. Financials n/a.
**New pattern: a living, well-resourced, institution-experienced founder (Cisco Systems co-founder) choosing the plainest possible pathway — personal sale, no foundation, no gift — for a collection about as tightly-coherent as this sample has coded.** A useful counter-example inside H4/H5: coherence alone did not produce an institutional pathway here, sharpening the belief to durability-once-public rather than the decision to institutionalize at all.
Sources: ARTnews · Ocula · Artsy.

## Batch 29 (2026-08-27, twenty-ninth run) — a founder who chooses no institutional wrapper at all, a billionaire's founding-moment governance recusal, and a surviving-husband dispersal across four institutions

**Process note.** WebFetch was re-tested this run against projects.propublica.org (the standing test) and www.guidestar.org (a domain last re-tried several runs ago) — both `EGRESS_BLOCKED`, confirming the block is still session-wide.
