### 99. Cookie Factory (Denver) — Denver, CO, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[cookie-factory-denver]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** [nearest fit, taxonomy strained] · founder **Amanda Precourt** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **founder-sole**, deliberately — no board, no 501(c)(3), no fundraising, stated as philosophy not necessity · **adapted-other** (converted fortune-cookie factory) · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open** (living founder).
Numbers: opened May 2025; all financials **n/a** — a privately held, privately funded venture with no nonprofit filing obligation of any kind, a structural (not tooling) gap.
**New pattern this case: "the eponymous museum is losing share to lighter models" (Pattern 3) taken to its logical extreme.** Every prior instance of a lighter model in this dataset is still a *nonprofit* choice — a foundation, a loan-only program. Precourt, backed by real family wealth (an energy-sector fortune), chose zero institutional wrapper of any kind, on record as a philosophical stance against "fundraising against other organizations." Closest comparator: [[bunker-artspace]] (a wealthy collector skipping the museum form), but Cookie Factory's founder states the anti-nonprofit choice explicitly and repeatedly, where Bunker Artspace's rationale was never detailed to that degree.
Sources: 5280 · Frieze · The Colorado Sun · Cultured · Denver7.

### 100. Powder Art Foundation (Powder Mountain) — Eden, UT, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[powder-art-foundation]]
pathway **found-art-park** · founder **Reed Hastings** living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **emerging** · durability **indeterminate** · governance **independent-board** (five-person board + ED; Hastings funded it, holds no official role) · **no-building** (open-air; a visitor center is planned, not built) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (site-specific outdoor sculpture/land art) · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: legal_recognition_year 2024 [secondary, EIN 99-4005610 via a GuideStar search snippet]; 6-7 installed works (Holzer, Holt, Turrell, McCarthy, Sekine, Hill, Ojo) as of 2026 [secondary]. Financials unknown (too new; no first 990 located, WebFetch blocked).
**New pattern this case: a founding-moment governance recusal, not a later correction.** Every independent-board survivor in this dataset until now arrived there either at founding by a founder with no heirs (Fondation Beyeler) or only after a crisis forced a handoff (Dia Art Foundation, Pyramid Hill). Hastings, by contrast, is a living billionaire founder who set up an independent board and stepped back from any official role **before** the institution even had its first work installed — the cleanest, fastest "governance design over endowment" data point this dataset has yet coded, though still untested by time. A second Dia Art Foundation appearance in the sample, this run as an external curatorial partner rather than the primary institution.
Sources: Robb Report · Artnet News · Galerie · Archpaper · Surface · The Salt Lake Tribune.

### 101. John Ashbery Collection & Archive (Flow Chart Foundation / Morgan / Beinecke / Harvard) — Hudson, NY + multi-site, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[ashbery-collection-flow-chart]]
pathway **hybrid** · `secondary_pathways` [donate-existing-museum-intact, found-foundation, intentional-dispersal] · founder **John Ashbery** (with David Kermani) deceased (d. 2017) · **survived_founder yes** (Flow Chart Foundation operating continuously since 1998, 9 years past his death) · outcome **stable** · durability **moderate** · governance **mixed** (Ashbery + Kermani + reportedly two other poets co-founded, not sole collector control) · **adapted-residence** (Hudson house, documented not toured) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** (works from Ashbery's own artist-friend circle) · decision **spouse** (Kermani, as literary executor) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **no**.

**New pattern this case: a `hybrid` pathway split by material type, not by ownership share.** [[stieglitz-collection-fisk-crystal-bridges]] (Batch 28) split ownership of one collection between two institutions; Ashbery splits by *kind of object* instead, papers and library to a university archive, works on paper to a museum, broader objects to a research library, house/legacy to a dedicated foundation, no single recipient holding "the collection." A third independent instance of the pre-death-structure pattern (foundation established 19 years before the founder's death) after [[pearlman-foundation]] and [[wolf-kahn-foundation]], and this dataset's cleanest test yet of `decision_owner: spouse` as the operative actor, directly relevant to [[market-intelligence]]'s "surviving spouse as first decision-maker" finding.
Sources: The Morgan Library & Museum · Beinecke Library, Yale · Harvard Library · The Flow Chart Foundation.

## Batch 38 (2026-08-31, thirty-eighth run, second run today) — a clean undisputed bequest and the corporate sample's first Southeast Asian case

**New cases:**
1. **[[charles-dee-mitchell-collection]]** — Charles Dee Mitchell, a Dallas art critic and former Half Price Books executive, signed a bequest in 2021 committing his 540-work collection (weighted toward photography, especially conflict photography, and works on paper) and his own East Dallas home to the Dallas Museum of Art; both arrived in 2026 following his November 2025 death, exactly as written, with no dispute, litigation, or contested figure located anywhere in coverage — a genuine counterweight to this dataset's more contested bequest cases (Star of Hope, Corcoran, Herbert Lust). `decision_owner: collector-alone`, `pathway: donate-existing-museum-intact`.
2. **[[uob-art-collection]]** — United Overseas Bank has collected Southeast Asian art since the early 1970s, explicitly to support a then-fledgling regional art scene; it now funds the region's longest-running art prize (UOB Painting of the Year, since 1982) and anchors the UOB Southeast Asia Gallery as a Founding Partner of National Gallery Singapore. Closes a real gap — the corporate sub-sample (eleven prior cases) was entirely US/European before this. Collection size is a genuine three-way source contradiction (2,600 / 2,800 / 8,000+ works), coded `unknown` per the template's contested-field rule rather than picking one figure. `origin: corporate`, `pathway: hybrid`.

**Process notes:** both sourced via a targeted WebSearch discovery pass, since [[candidate-universe]]'s prior pool was confirmed fully absorbed as of Batch 37's "Discovery cycle 3" note; Mitchell was already sitting as an uncoded row in the 2026-08-31 Signals section (flagged the day before this run but not yet converted to a full case). A third draft this run, the Wolf Kahn Foundation & Archives (a 2026-08-24 Signals row that had also sat uncoded for a week), was written in full and then found, via the standing `ls cases/*.md` / `grep "status: coded"` coverage check, to duplicate an already-coded case, [[wolf-kahn-foundation]] — deleted before being counted, and [[candidate-universe]]'s own stale un-CODED marker for that row corrected in the same pass. `candidate-universe.md` was updated to mark the Mitchell row CODED and to add a new "Discovery cycle 4" section documenting UOB's discovery plus two considered-but-not-coded leads (HSBC's larger, more diffuse global collection; Citi Private Bank's Art Advisory practice, which turned out to be this run's Part D disconfirmation-hunt subject instead of a case). WebFetch re-tested against projects.propublica.org and remained `EGRESS_BLOCKED`; both cases are `Provisional`/`secondary`, neither filing a directly-fetched US 990.

## Batch 37 (2026-08-31, thirty-seventh run) — a record-scale tax-driven donation, a corporate collection's disposition plan reversed by a CEO succession, and an artist foundation upgraded from a bare claims-register mention

**New cases:**
- [[lee-kun-hee-collection]] — Seoul, South Korea. The Samsung family's 2021 donation of 23,181 works (Korean antiquities through Monet, Picasso, Chagall, Dalí) to the National Museum of Korea and MMCA, made alongside a separately paid ~$10.8B cash inheritance tax bill, the largest in Korean history. `pathway: donate-existing-museum-scattered`, `origin: corporate`, `outcome: stable`. Widely credited with driving Korea's own 2022/2023 art-in-lieu-of-tax law reform, which the donation itself predates and did not use. A genuinely large gap this dataset had not coded despite the case's scale and citability.
- [[unicredit-art-collection]] — Milan, Italy (pan-European). A 60,000-plus-work bank collection whose 2019 CEO-driven "Art4Future" sell-off plan (Richter, Klein, Nam June Paik sold via Christie's) was halted entirely in 2021 by a successor CEO citing heritage value, no financial motive stated for either decision. `pathway: abandoned-plan`, `origin: corporate`, `outcome: pivoted`. The corporate sub-sample's first pure leadership-succession reversal — every prior corporate case here (Lehman, Enron, WestLB/Portigon, Deutsche Bank) is driven by external financial pressure; this one is driven by nothing but a change of chief executive.
- [[hilma-af-klint-foundation]] — Stockholm, Sweden. A 1972 artist foundation whose founding statutes require both family chairmanship and an Anthroposophical Society board majority, embedding the artist's own spiritualist intent directly into governance rather than as a separate written instruction. `pathway: found-foundation`, `origin: artist`, `outcome: distressed` (coded on a 2023-2026 litigated board conflict, not financial distress). Upgraded from a bare [[claims-register]] mention (C47) to a full case; this run's research found the May 2026 settlement ends the litigation but does not confirm the underlying David Zwirner gallery-partnership deal's own fate — C47's prior "board-resolved, kept publicly accessible" framing is corrected in both the claims register and this run's manuscript update.

**How these were found:** a coverage check (`ls cases/*.md` cross-referenced against [[candidate-universe]]) found the entire prior candidate pool already coded — 127 case files, only [[mcnay]] itself not yet `status: coded`. Rather than force-code a stale or thin candidate, this run ran a dedicated WebSearch discovery pass (corporate art collection news, private foundation dispute news, tax-and-legacy mechanism news) to find genuinely new, well-documented cases. All three found this way cleared the bar on first pass; no false starts or reverted drafts this run. [[candidate-universe]] itself was updated with a new "Discovery cycle 3" section for these three finds, plus an explicit pool-status note for future runs.

**This run's Primary-verification backlog:** unchanged at 75 items. None of this run's three new cases were added — all three carry a structural, not tooling, disclosure gap: a Korean state-museum donation has no US-style filing to pull; a for-profit Italian bank's owned asset was never subject to nonprofit disclosure at all; a Swedish stiftelse (foundation) is not required to file anything comparable to a US Form 990.

## Batch 36 (2026-08-30, thirty-sixth run, second run today) — a civic, non-eponymous museum built through nine decades of separate bequests, the smallest-scale art park coded to date, and a taxonomy-deferral held rather than forced

**New cases:**
- [[mint-museum]] — Charlotte, NC. Founded 1936 by an uncoordinated citizens' coalition (led by Mary Myers Dwelle), not a private collector, after buying the original 1837 US Mint building from the US Treasury for $950 and moving it to a new site. Grown for nine decades through a sequence of separate, unconnected individual bequests (M. Mellanay Delhom's ceramics purchase, 1965; the Robicsek gift of ancient Americas material; more recent Belk-Cook and Bellios bequests), none tied to any coordinating figure located this run. Coded conservatively `outcome_category: stable` (not `thriving`) on a real, sourced FY2024 operating deficit (~$8.98M revenue against ~$14.1M expenses, even as total assets grew to ~$95M) — a civic, always-independent-board institution nonetheless producing an institutional-economics "money" pressure of the kind Pattern 2 already tracks for founder-sole failures.
- [[price-sculpture-forest]] — Coupeville, WA (Whidbey Island). The smallest-scale `found-art-park` case coded to date (~$221K total assets, files Form 990-PF). Living founder Scott Price converted a 2008 land purchase, originally meant for a family home, into a free public sculpture park (opened October 2020) rather than let the land be sold and likely cleared. Its distinctive feature is a permanent conservation easement with the Whidbey Camano Land Trust binding the *land* itself against development, independent of the nonprofit's own governance or finances — see new Pattern 15, above.

**Process note — a taxonomy decision held, not forced.** This run initially drafted a third full case file for Art Omi Pavilions (Chatham, NY), the multi-collector shared-campus consortium flagged in [[candidate-universe]] as needing a possible seventh pathway family. Before committing it, a check of [[candidate-universe]]'s own entry found an explicit standing instruction: the case file stays deferred until Alina resolves [[pathway-family-proposal-shared-campus]], a taxonomy decision reserved for her because it would touch the export schema, the coded-header controlled vocabulary, and the public site's decision pages. The drafted file was deleted before being counted or committed. The research gathered in drafting it (confirmation that the project runs through a dedicated new entity, Art Omi Pavilions at Chatham Inc., EIN 88-4167493, a joint venture between Art Omi Inc. and Francis Greenburger's own foundation; a ~$60M total project cost with only ~$7M publicly disclosed; a still-unresolved Spring/June 2027 opening-date conflict across sources; and the still-unanswered question of whether participating collectors retain legal title to their own pavilion's contents) was banked as a dated addendum to the proposal memo instead, so the eventual decision starts from a fuller base without this run overriding a decision explicitly not its own to make.

A second candidate draft, for the Fisher Landau Center for Art, was also not counted: the standing `ls`/`grep` coverage check found it already coded as [[fisher-landau]] (already item #4 in this file's own Primary-verification backlog, and already cited in Pattern 14's governance-table caveat). [[candidate-universe]]'s own CODED-marker was simply out of date for it, corrected this run alongside the two new cases and Price Sculpture Forest's own marker.

**This run's Primary-verification backlog:** grows from 73 to **75 items** — [[mint-museum]] and [[price-sculpture-forest]] added as items 74-75. Both file ordinary US Form 990/990-PF returns; the gap is tooling (WebFetch blocked), not structural.

pathway **found-foundation** · `secondary_pathways` [abandoned-plan] · founder **Robert Indiana** deceased (d. 2018) · **survived_founder yes** (Foundation operating 8 years past his death) · outcome **pivoted** · durability **moderate** · governance **founder-sole → independent-board** (formalized post-death) · **adapted-other** (a former Odd Fellows lodge) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** · decision **foundation-executive** · **friction funding-gap** · **constraints_documented: yes** — the single most important field: Indiana's will directed the Lodge be restored "to museum quality" and opened to the public; the Foundation he set up to do exactly that instead voted, eight years after his death and with no reported litigation, to sell it.

### 114. HSBC Art Collection — London, UK (global) · Provisional · full note [[hsbc-art-collection]]
pathway **hybrid** · origin **corporate** · founder **n/a** (first acquisition 1923, then-chief manager Alexander Stephen) · **survived_founder n/a** · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **parent-institution** · **existing-institution** · coherence **coherent-multi** · decision **no-identifiable-person** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **n/a**.

### 115. Fondation Pierre Bergé – Yves Saint Laurent / Jardin Majorelle — Paris, France / Marrakech, Morocco · Spot-verified · full note [[fondation-pierre-berge-yves-saint-laurent]]
pathway **hybrid** · `secondary_pathways` [found-foundation, sell-auction, long-term-loan] · founders **Yves Saint Laurent & Pierre Bergé**, both deceased (YSL 2008, Bergé 2017) · **survived_founder yes** (24 years operating, 18 past YSL's death, 9 past Bergé's) · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **founder-sole** at 2002 founding → **mixed** by Bergé's 2017 death (named multi-member board) · **purpose-built** (Musée YSL Paris, 2017) plus **adapted-other** (Jardin Majorelle) · coherence **tight-single-thesis** for the Foundation's own holdings, but the *auctioned* personal collection was **broad-survey** · decision **spouse** (Bergé, as PACS partner and co-founder, chose to auction the couple's separate personal collection) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **no**.

**New pattern: a founding couple deliberately routing two adjacent legacies to two different pathways at the same historical moment** — the couture archive into a pre-built foundation, the separate personal art collection into total, planned auction, proceeds funding the foundation. This dataset's first same-sex-partnership succession case (a PACS civil union formalized days before Saint Laurent's death) and a `decision_owner: spouse` shape distinct from [[weisman]] (institutionalizing) and [[de-la-cruz-collection]] (discretionary liquidation with no institutional intermediary) — here the spouse's discretionary sale decision fed directly back into an institution the couple had already built together.

Full result, including the direct relevance to this run's own [[yemisi-shyllon-museum-of-art]] case (a founder pre-funding operating costs specifically against this risk), in [[what-we-now-believe]]'s new dated entry.

Monte dei Paschi's 2013-2017 near-total institutional failure (a 68% state ownership stake) left its 500+-year art collection untouched — a real, narrowing disconfirmation: the operative variable is the specific legal mechanism distress resolves through (liquidation reaches the art; recapitalization does not), not distress severity alone. Corcoran's mechanism (exhaustion with no rescuer available) stands undisturbed. Full result, including the open question of whether this holds outside a "too big to fail" case, in [[what-we-now-believe]]'s new dated entry and the H6/H8 evidence-base entry (twenty-second hunt).

## Batch 44 (2026-09-03, forty-fourth run, second run today) — a founderless institution stalled by contested authority, and two new geographies

**Process note.** Following on from this morning's Batch 43, this run closed the MOWAA lead that batch's own discovery pass had flagged but not coded, once a fresh search confirmed the dispute had reached a datable, codeable outcome (a November 2025 protest and a land-title revocation, not an open-ended "engulfed in disputes" description). A second WebSearch discovery pass, targeting two geographies with zero prior representation (Turkey, the Philippines), found the other two cases. ### 142. Museum of West African Art (MOWAA) — Benin City, Nigeria · Spot-verified · full note [[mowaa]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · `secondary_pathways` [abandoned-plan] · `pathway_is_branched` yes · founder **n/a** (institutional, no collector-founder) · **survived_founder n/a** · outcome **distressed** · durability **weak** · governance **independent-board** (Legacy Restoration Trust, UK charity, multiple institutional co-funders) · **purpose-built** · coherence **n/a** · decision **no-identifiable-person** · **friction community-opposition** (+ legal-challenge) · constraints **open**.
Numbers: ~$25M+ raised from international donors including a $3M Mellon Foundation grant (2024); financials otherwise `unknown` — Legacy Restoration Trust's UK charity number was not located this run, added to the Primary-verification backlog as item 64 (a resolvable, not structural, gap — a UK Charity Commission filing exists in principle).
**New pattern: this dataset's first case with no individual collector-founder to code against at all**, and a genuinely new failure mechanism. Every prior founder-museum failure ([[terra]], [[di-rosa]]) traces to a governance vacuum or funding exhaustion inside the institution itself. MOWAA has neither — a dedicated charity board, named international institutional funders (British Museum, German government, Mellon Foundation), and a marquee architect (Sir David Adjaye) — and stalled anyway, when a November 2025 protest asserting the Oba of Benin's traditional authority over the Benin Bronzes disrupted the public-opening preview and Edo State revoked the project's land rights. A disconfirmation-relevant case: independent, professional, well-funded governance is not sufficient for durability when a party entirely outside that governance structure holds a competing, unresolved claim to the underlying cultural patrimony.
Sources: Hyperallergic · Artforum · ARTnews · Museums Association · allAfrica · African Business · Financial Street.

Also a clean pre-death lock-in (bequest 1998, three years before his 2004 death), extending Pattern 1 to a case where the lock-in mechanism is a purpose-built parent institution rather than an existing one.
Sources: Sabancı University · Sakıp Sabancı Museum (SSM) · Sotheby's museum profile · Wikipedia.

Export regenerated: `python scripts/export_public.py` → see the export result below. [[sakip-sabanci-museum]] and [[pinto-art-museum]] both carry structural, not tooling, disclosure gaps (Turkey and the Philippines have no US-990-equivalent regime reachable by this run's tooling) and are flagged in their own files rather than the numbered list, per the convention already established for [[muzej-lah]] and [[museum-macan]].

**Part D — the twenty-fifth deliberate disconfirmation pass.** Built directly around this run's new [[mowaa]] case, testing an assumption implicit in H6/H8's governance finding but never directly stated: that independent, professional, multi-stakeholder governance is *sufficient* for durability, the way it has so far proven *necessary* (Terra's founder-sole collapse, Corcoran's slower financial exhaustion). MOWAA has governance at least as independent and professionally credentialed as any confirming case in the evidence base — a dedicated charity board, no single controlling individual, named institutional co-funders including the British Museum and the German government, adequate reported funding for construction — and it stalled anyway, via a mechanism no prior case has shown: a party with no board seat or governance role at all (a traditional monarch, backed by a state government's land-title revocation) asserting a competing claim over the underlying cultural patrimony and land the institution sits on. This is a real, if narrow, disconfirmation: good governance predicts survival against the *internal* failure modes this evidence base has tested repeatedly (founder-death vacuum, funding exhaustion, financial-distress liquidation) but says nothing about, and offers no protection against, an *external* contest over legitimacy or title that never touches the institution's own board at all. Full write-up in [[what-we-now-believe]]'s new dated entry and the H6/H8 evidence-base entry (twenty-fifth hunt).

### 136. Mennello Museum of American Art — Orlando, FL, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[mennello-museum]]
pathway **found-standalone-museum** · founder **Marilyn & Michael Mennello**, deceased (2006, 2020) · **survived_founder yes** · outcome **stable** · durability **moderate** · governance **parent-institution** (City of Orlando) · **adapted-residence** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **no**.
Numbers: 35 founding-gift Earl Cunningham paintings (1998) plus a 2018 gift of 14 paintings/5 sculptures ($8.75M appraised); Friends of the Mennello Museum (support nonprofit, EIN 59-3618760) reports $2.08M total assets against $355K expenses on $306K revenue, FY2024 [secondary, aggregator-cited] — added to the Primary-verification backlog as item 78.
**New pattern: the sample's first museum owned by a municipal government from its founding**, not a university or a family/foundation vehicle — a third distinct flavor of `governance_control_at_founding: parent-institution`, and a case whose located financial disclosure (a support nonprofit's 990) does not actually cover the museum's real operating budget, a genuinely new disclosure-shape wrinkle.
Sources: Mennello Museum of American Art (Wikipedia) · Orlando Sentinel · Orlando Weekly · GuideStar/990-aggregator summary.

### 137. Jill and Sheldon Bonovitz Collection — Philadelphia, PA, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[bonovitz-collection]]
pathway **donate-existing-museum-intact** · founder **Jill & Sheldon Bonovitz**, living · **survived_founder not-yet-testable** · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **independent-board** (receiving institution, PMA) · **existing-institution** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** · decision **collector-alone** · **friction none-documented** · constraints **open**.
Numbers: 200+ works by 27 self-taught American artists [secondary]; promised gift formalized 2011; no dedicated foundation or 990 vehicle of any kind exists for the collection.
**New pattern: a ninth H2 insider-exception confirmation** (Sheldon Bonovitz sits on both the Philadelphia Museum of Art's and the Barnes Foundation's boards of trustees) **and a distinct disclosure shape** — unlike every other `donate-existing-museum` case in this sample, there is no intermediate foundation or filing entity of any kind between the collectors and the receiving museum, a structurally-unresolvable financial gap rather than a WebFetch-tooling one.
Sources: Philadelphia Museum of Art · Duane Morris (attorney profile) · The Philadelphia Inquirer · Wharton Magazine.

### 138. Joy of Giving Something (Howard Stein photography collection) — New York, NY, USA · Spot-verified · full note [[joy-of-giving-something]]
pathway **intentional-dispersal** · `secondary_pathways` [found-foundation] · founder **Howard Stein**, deceased (2011) · **survived_founder yes** · outcome **thriving** · durability **strong** · governance **founder-sole** [inferred] · **no-building** · coherence **tight-single-thesis** · decision **spouse** (Janet Stein) · **friction none-documented** · constraints **partial**.
Numbers: net assets $39.6M (FY2024, aggregator-cited 990 figure) — added to the Primary-verification backlog as item 79; 1,986 photographs gifted to the Virginia Museum of Fine Arts alone in 2026; total original collection size unconfirmed.
**New pattern: a foundation created 13 years pre-death (1998) that did not begin its actual dispersal program until 6 years post-death (2017)**, still actively placing works in 2026 — a real gap between structure-in-place and pathway-executed, and a `decision_owner: spouse` case where a surviving spouse and trustees, not a founder's own pre-set instrument, drove the eventual disposition. Worth reading alongside [[pearlman-foundation]]'s own multi-decade resolution timeline as a second data point on how long even a deliberate pre-death structure can take to actually execute.
Sources: jgsfoundation.org · The Art Newspaper · ARTnews · Robb Report · 990-aggregator summary.

**Public-projection field backfill this run:** all three new cases carry full public fields from coding; the rest of the coded set remains complete (confirmed via `grep -L "^public_page_eligible" cases/*.md`, only `mcnay.md` — `status: founder-review`, not `coded` — lacks them). Export regenerated: `python scripts/export_public.py` → see the export result below.

**This run's Primary-verification backlog:** grows from 77 to **79 items** — [[mennello-museum]] added as item 78 (EIN 59-3618760, a support-nonprofit filing, not the museum's own budget), [[joy-of-giving-something]] added as item 79 (EIN 13-4033237). [[bonovitz-collection]] carries a structural, not tooling, gap of a different shape than usual — not a missing national disclosure regime, but no dedicated filing vehicle of any kind — and is flagged in its own file rather than the numbered list, per the convention [[hsbc-art-collection]] and [[malba-costantini]] already established for structural gaps.

**Part D — the twenty-third deliberate disconfirmation pass: a full in-house recount, not a fresh external search.** Rather than hunt for one more external counter-case on an already heavily-tested belief, this run tried to break H3 ("standalone founder-museums survive at the same rate as lighter models") by re-running its own original test — full `pathway` × `outcome` count across ALL coded cases — now that the dataset has grown from the 70 cases the finding was first drawn from (2026-08-21) to 138, almost exactly double. Excluding `emerging`/`pivoted`/`founder-open` (too-new-to-judge or founder-still-active) cases, the same exclusion logic the original test used: **built-institution pathways** (found-standalone-museum + found-house-museum + found-art-park, n=48 total, 33 definitive-outcome) now show a **93.9% thriving/stable rate (31/33)** — the two failures still just [[terra]] and [[di-rosa]]. **Lighter models** (long-term-loan + traveling-program + intentional-dispersal, n=14 total, 13 definitive-outcome) show **92.3% (12/13)** — the one failure [[pearlman-foundation]]. The gap between the two narrowed from ~2 points (92% vs. 90%, n=25/n=10) to ~1.6 points (93.9% vs. 92.3%, n=33/n=13) at roughly double the sample size — a failed disconfirmation attempt that *strengthens* confidence in the original finding through replication, not one that breaks it. **A genuinely new, unplanned finding surfaced from the same recount**: the dataset's fast-growing `hybrid` pathway (branched, multi-path cases — now 26 total, up from effectively zero in the original 70-case test, driven by this dataset's own growing sophistication at coding branched dispositions) shows a materially LOWER definitive-outcome success rate, **78.9% (15/19)** — four failures ([[brauer-museum-valparaiso]], [[cam-raleigh]], [[fisher-landau]], [[herbert-lust-collection]]) against 15 successes. This was not the belief under test and is not yet a hypothesis, only a pattern worth naming for a future run: cases whose disposition branches across multiple pathways may fail at a meaningfully higher rate than cases with one clean pathway, possibly because branching itself is sometimes a symptom of an unresolved, contested, or financially strained transition rather than a deliberate design choice (Fisher Landau and CAM Raleigh both show real financial/founder-death pressure in their own case files). Full write-up, including the honest caveat that this remains a hand-picked, diversity-selected sample rather than a random draw, in [[what-we-now-believe]]'s new dated entry and the H3 evidence-base entry.
